RATIONAL CHOW RING OF THE UNIVERSAL MODULI SPACE OF SEMISTABLE RANK TWO BUNDLES OVER GENUS TWO CURVES
Shubham Saha

Abstract
. We determine the rational Chow ring of the universal moduli space of rank 2 2 semistable bundles over smooth curves of genus 2 2 , and show that it is generated by certain tautological classes. In the process, we obtain Chow rings of universal Jacobians over genus 2 2 curves with marked Weierstrass points, and the Chow ring of the universal pointed Jacobian. This further provides alternate computations to [ 24 ] in the genus 2 2 case.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
14C17, 14D20, 14H45

1.
Introduction

The universal moduli stack of vector bundles over algebraic curves has been the focus of extensive study in
[
3
,
14
,
15
,
24
,
26
,
36
]
among others.
Notably, the universal Picard stack has emerged as a central object in the intersection theory of the moduli space of curves, particularly through its connection to the double ramification cycle
[
36
]
.
Despite these developments, the global geometry of these stacks, in rank one or higher, and their associated good moduli spaces remains only partially understood.
Aside from computations of their Picard groups
[
14
,
24
,
26
]
; and Chow rings of universal Picard stacks over the hyperelliptic locus
[
24
]
, much of their intersection-theoretic properties are still unknown in higher ranks (beyond genus
0
where the Chow ring was calculated in
[
23
]
).

Let
\mathscr{U}(r,d,g)\xrightarrow{}\mathcal{M}_{g}
be the stack of semistable bundles of rank
r
, degree
d
over smooth curves of genus
g
. The stack
\mathscr{U}(r,d,g)
is a smooth Artin stack of finite type over
\mathcal{M}_{g}

[
14
, Theorem 1.2.2]
with a good moduli space
U(r,d,g)\xrightarrow{}{M}_{g}
.

Much like
\mathcal{M}_{g}
, we can define tautological classes on the moduli stacks of bundles over curves. For notational convenience, let
\mathcal{E}_{d}
be the Poincaré bundle on

\mathscr{C}_{g}\times_{\mathcal{M}_{g}}\mathscr{U}(r,d,g)\xrightarrow{\pi_{\mathscr{U}}}\mathscr{U}(r,d,g)
where
\mathscr{C}_{g}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{g}
is the universal curve.
The
twisted-kappa classes
have been defined in
[
24
]
for the universal Picard stack and the definition can be adapted to higher ranks as discussed in §
2.4
.

We compute the rational Chow ring of the good moduli space
U(2,d,2)
for all values of
d
.
Due to the Artin-stack nature of
\mathscr{U}(2,d,2)
, the natural pullback
A^{*}({U}(2,d,2))\hookrightarrow A^{*}(\mathscr{U}(2,d,2))
fails to be an isomorphism.

We explicitly describe this map by expressing the images of the generators of
A^{*}(U(2,d,2))
in terms of the tautological classes on
\mathscr{U}(2,d,2)
.

For even degree, the canonical isomorphisms between
\mathscr{U}(2,2d,2)
for different values of
d
with the same parity, obtained by suitable twisting of the relative canonical, reduce the problem to the cases
d=0,1
.

Theorem A
.

There are divisors
\nu_{d},\tilde{Z}_{d}\in A^{1}(U(2,2d,2))\forall d\in\mathbb{Z}
, such that

A^{*}(U(2,2d,2))\simeq\mathbb{Q}[\nu_{d},\tilde{Z}_{d}]/(\nu_{d}^{4},\tilde{Z}_{d}^{3}).

The image of the pullback
A^{*}(U(2,2d,2))\rightarrow A^{*}(\mathscr{U}(2,2d,2))
for
d\in\{0,1\}
is given by

\nu_{d}\mapsto\kappa_{-1,0,1}-\dfrac{1}{4}\kappa_{-1,2,0},\tilde{Z}_{d}\mapsto 4d\kappa_{0,1,0}-2\kappa_{-1,2,0}.

As a byproduct of these computations, we recover
[
24
, Theorem 1.1]
for genus
2
, and the rational Picard group as presented in
[
14
, Theorem A.2]
for the rank
2
, genus
2
case.

For all values of
d
,
A^{*}(U(2,2d,2))
are shown to be isomorphic despite the lack of canonical isomorphisms between
U(2,0,2),U(2,2,2)
.
However, we show that the images of pullbacks

A^{*}(U(2,2d,2))\rightarrow A^{*}(\mathscr{U}(2,2d,2))

have different structures based on the parity of
d
.

Corollary A.1
.

The class of the strictly semistable locus in
A^{*}(U(2,2d,2))
is given by
4\nu_{d}\in A^{1}(U(2,2d,2))
. Applying excision, we have

A^{*}(U^{s}(2,2d,2))\simeq\mathbb{Q}[\tilde{Z}_{d}]/(\tilde{Z}_{d}^{3}).

For odd degree, we have canonical isomorphisms between
\mathscr{U}(2,2d-1,2)
for different values of
d
, obtained by suitable twisting of the relative canonical and taking duals, based on the parity of
d
.

This reduces the problem to the case
d=3
. The specific choice
d=3
is numerically motivated as it makes the description of the generators in the Chow ring simpler and allows the application of tools described in
[
5
]
.

Theorem B
.

Let
B_{*}\in A^{2}(U(2,3,2))
be the universal Brill-Noether locus of bundles with
2
independent sections.
There are divisors
H_{U},\Theta_{U}\in A^{1}(U(2,3,2))
such that

\displaystyle A^{*}(\mathcal{U}(2,3,2))\simeq A^{*}(\tilde{U}(2,3,2))=\mathbb{Q}[H_{U},\Theta_{U},B_{*}]/(\Theta_{U}^{3},H_{U}^{2}\Theta_{U}^{2}-4B_{*}\Theta_{U}^{2},B_{*}H_{U}^{2}-B_{*}H_{U}\Theta_{U}+\dfrac{1}{2}B_{*}\Theta_{U}^{2},

\displaystyle B_{*}H_{U}^{2}-B_{*}^{2},H_{U}^{3}+H_{U}^{2}\Theta_{U}+\dfrac{1}{2}H_{U}\Theta_{U}^{2}-4B_{*}H_{U}).

The image of the pullback
A^{*}(U(2,3,2))\rightarrow A^{*}(\mathscr{U}(2,3,2))
is given by

\displaystyle H_{U}\mapsto\dfrac{1}{2}\left(4\kappa_{-1,0,1}-\kappa_{-1,2,0}\right),B_{*}\mapsto\dfrac{1}{2}\left(\dfrac{1}{2}(\kappa_{-1,2,0}-\kappa_{0,1,0})-\kappa_{-1,0,1}\right)^{2}-\dfrac{1}{2}\left(\dfrac{\kappa_{-1,3,0}}{3}-{\kappa_{-1,1,1}}-\dfrac{\kappa_{0,2,0}}{2}+{\kappa_{0,0,1}}\right),

\displaystyle\Theta_{U}\mapsto\dfrac{1}{2}\left(3\kappa_{0,1,0}-\kappa_{-1,2,0}\right).

We consider
M_{*}
, the coarse moduli space of
\mathcal{H}^{w}_{2,6}
(stack of genus
2
curves with marked Weierstrass points). It is known that
\mathcal{H}^{w}_{2,6}
is a trivial
\mu_{2}
-gerbe over
M_{*}
by
[
11
, Proposition 6.3]
.
The associated section
M_{*}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{w}_{2,6}
gives a “universal” family of genus
2
curves
\mathscr{C}_{*}\xrightarrow{\pi}M_{*}
with sections
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq 6}
parametrizing Weierstrass points.
For degree
d
, the relative Jacobian for the family
\pi
is denoted by
J_{*}(d)
. Our computations of
A^{*}(J_{*}(d))
show that the pullback map
A^{*}(J_{2}^{d})\rightarrow A^{*}(J_{*}(d))
is an isomorphism for all values of
d
where
J_{2}^{d}
is the universal Jacobian of degree
d
over genus
2
curves.

Theorem C
.

The Chow ring of
J_{*}(d)
is given by

A^{*}(J_{*}(d))\simeq\mathbb{Q}[\Theta_{d}]/(\Theta_{d}^{3})

for all
d\in\mathbb{Z}
. Consequently, the Chow rings of
J_{2}^{d}
are given by

A^{*}(J_{2}^{1})\simeq\mathbb{Q}[\Theta]/(\Theta^{3}),A^{*}(J_{2}^{2})\simeq\mathbb{Q}[Z]/(Z^{3})

where
\Theta
is the theta-divisor in
J_{2}^{1}
and
Z\hookrightarrow J_{2}^{2}
is the image of
Z_{\mathscr{C}}
under the map
J_{*}(2)\rightarrow J_{2}^{2}
.

Additionally, the computations for
A^{*}(U(2,3,2))
uses the Chow ring of
C_{2}\times_{M_{2}}J_{2}^{d}
where
C_{2}\rightarrow M_{2}
is the universal curve over
M_{2}
. To simplify notation, we compute the image of its pullback to
A^{*}(\mathscr{C}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(3))
. We further denote
\mathscr{C}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(3)
by
\mathscr{C}_{J}
.

Theorem D
.

Let
S^{*}(\mathscr{C}_{J})
be the image of the pullback
q_{C}^{*}:A^{*}(C_{2}\times_{M_{2}}J_{2}^{d})\hookrightarrow A^{*}(\mathscr{C}_{J})
. We have

S^{*}(\mathscr{C}_{J})\simeq\mathbb{Q}[K_{\pi_{J}},\pi_{J}^{*}\Theta_{*},\xi]/(\pi_{J}^{*}\Theta_{*}^{3},K_{\pi_{J}}^{2},\xi^{2}+K_{\pi_{J}}\pi_{J}^{*}\Theta_{*},\xi K_{\pi_{J}},(\xi-\dfrac{3}{2}K_{\pi_{J}})\pi_{J}^{*}\Theta_{*}^{2}).

1.1.
Outline of the paper

The foundational strategy of this paper is to extend the classical results in
[
5
]
and
[
28
]
to
{U}(2,d,2)
, from its fiber over the universal Jacobian.

We use
\pi
to construct
\tilde{U}(2,d,2)
, a finite cover of
U(2,d,2)
, and compute the image of the pullback map
A^{*}(U(2,d,2))\rightarrow A^{*}(\tilde{U}(2,d,2))
.

1.1.1.
Even Degree

We begin by setting up the problem in §
3
by defining the necessary spaces and morphisms.
Following the necessary constructions, we extend classical results from
[
28
]
to the universal setting in §
3.1
.
The calculation of the Chow rings of universal Jacobians and their defined covers are in §
3.2
.
Previous computations and arguments are applied in §
3.3
to compute
A^{*}(U(2,2d,2))
for
d=0,1
.
The universal moduli spaces of stable bundles are studied in §
3.4
.
We find explicit expressions for the seemingly abstract generators in terms of tautological classes by computing their pullbacks over a universal extension space with a “Poincaré bundle” in §
5.1
.

1.1.2.
Odd Degree

We use the succesive blowup of extension spaces described in
[
5
]
for degree
3
bundles.
The map from the blowup of the extension space to the moduli space is a blowdown along a Brill-Noether locus.
The geometry of this blowup-blowdown configuration is studied in §
4.1
.
To explictly carry out the constructions in the universal setting, we extend Bertram’s arguments to families of genus
2
curves with a line bundle of relative degree
3
in §
4.2
.
This is followed by certain computations in the Chow ring of the aforementioned blowup, namely
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\xi}
, which blows down to
\tilde{U}(2,3,2)
in §
4.3
.
We use the sections
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq 6}
to define several loci in §
4.4
to understand the blowdown
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\xi}\xrightarrow{\Phi_{\xi}}\tilde{U}(2,3,2)
. Specifically, we find the classes contracted by
\Phi_{\xi}
.
The linear generators of the Chow ring of the universal Brill-Noether locus, namely
B_{*}
, are constructed in §
4.5
.

We use the computations from §
4.3
, §
4.4
, §
4.5
, and an excision argument in §
4.6
to compute
A^{*}(U(2,3,2))
.
The final theorem states generators and relations to describe
A^{*}(U(2,3,2))
via its pullback into
A^{*}(\tilde{U}(2,3,2))
.
We find explicit expressions for the seemingly abstract generators in terms of tautological classes via their pullback to
\mathscr{U}(2,3,2)
in §
5.2
.

Notation

All schemes in this paper are taken over the field of complex numbers.
All Chow rings are taken with rational coefficients. We use the subspace convention for projective bundles.
For a fiber product
X\times_{S}Y
, we denote the projection map onto the
i
-th factor as
\pi_{i}
for
i=1,2
.

Remark
.

The underlying assumptions on the ground field, as outlined above, are used throughout the paper to establish certain isomorphisms via Theorem
2.1
.

2.
Background and known results

Zariski’s Main Theorem, as stated in
[
18
, Theorem 4.4.3]
, can be used to find a sufficient condition for schemes to be isomorphic over an algebraically closed field of characteristic
0
.

We use the following theorem throughout the paper to establish isomorphisms between varieties. A variety in this context is a reduced scheme of finite-type.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a morphism of quasi-projective varieties over an algebraically closed field of characteristic
0
. Then
f
is an isomorphism if the following conditions are satisfied:

•

f
is a bijection on closed points

•

Y
is normal

•

X
is connected.

2.1.
A cover of
{M}_{2}

Let
M_{*}=\mathcal{M}_{0,6}\simeq\{(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})|\quad p_{i}\in\mathbb{A}^{1}\setminus\{0,1\}\forall i;p_{i}\text{ mutually distinct}\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{3}
. The map
\mathcal{M}_{2}\rightarrow D_{6}=[M_{*}/S_{6}]
is a
\mu_{2}
-gerbe by
[
11
, Remark 2.1]
.
Hence the corresponding coarse moduli spaces for
\mathcal{M}_{2},D_{6}
are isomorphic. That is,
M_{2}\simeq M_{*}/S_{6}
where
M_{*}/S_{6}
is the GIT-quotient of the quasi-projective
M_{*}
.

Therefore, we have a morphism
M_{*}\rightarrow M_{2}
which makes
M_{*}
an étale
S_{6}
-cover of
M_{2}
. We can in fact show that the morphism
M_{*}\rightarrow M_{2}
lifts to
\mathcal{M}_{2}
. That is, there is a “universal” family of curves on
M_{*}
.

To construct such a family, we use arguments presented in
[
40
, Proposition 3.1]
. We make use of the following sections for the projection
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times M_{*}\rightarrow M_{*}
:

\sigma_{1}={0}\times M_{*},\sigma_{2}={1}\times M_{*},\sigma_{3}={\infty}\times M_{*},(\sigma_{i+3})_{(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})}=p_{i}\forall 1\leq i\leq 3

We construct the family
\mathscr{C}\rightarrow M_{*}
by setting

\mathscr{C}_{*}:=\underline{Proj}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}\times M_{*}}(\mathcal{O}\oplus\mathcal{O}(-3)),

where the product on this sheaf of rings is given by the map
\mathcal{O}(-6)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{O}
,
defined by the Cartier divisor
D_{\sigma}:=\Sigma_{1\leq i\leq 6}\sigma_{i}
.
