Now,
\phi
is a degree
2
cover ramified along
\sigma_{i}
’s. Using Theorem
2.1
,
(\phi^{-1}(\sigma_{i}))_{red}
maps isomorphically to
M_{*}
via
\pi
.
Therefore, the sections
\sigma_{i}
lift to sections of
\mathscr{C}_{*}\rightarrow M_{*}
. We identify
\sigma_{i}
with its lift to
\mathscr{C}_{*}
.

Furthermore, we have a moduli interpretation of
M_{*}
. Following the notation in
[
11
]
, the map
\mathcal{H}_{2,6}^{w}\rightarrow M_{*}
is a
\mu_{2}
-gerbe. Therefore,
M_{*}
is the coarse moduli space to the stack of genus
2
curves with marked Weierstrass points.

Remark 1
.

The induced
S_{6}
-action on
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times M_{*}\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}\times M_{0,6}
(see
[
11
, Proposition 6.1]
for details) permutes the sections
(\sigma_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 6}
accordingly.
Hence, the divisor
D_{\sigma}
is invariant under this action.

Let
\mathcal{R}:=\mathcal{O}\oplus\mathcal{O}(-3)\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}\times M_{*}
. We thus have natural isomorphisms
g^{*}\mathcal{R}\simeq\mathcal{R}\forall g\in S_{6}
.
These isomorphisms induce an
S_{6}
-action on
\mathscr{C}_{*}
with the claimed properties.
We can therefore define an
S_{6}
-action on
\mathscr{C}_{*}
so that
\pi
is equivariant.

2.2.
Chow Rings and Stacks

Much like homology, there is an excision sequence for Chow groups. This is used in the paper to obtain generators for Chow groups under a given stratification.

Theorem 2.2
.

Let
X
be a scheme and
Z\xrightarrow{i_{Z}}X
be a closed subscheme. Let
U:=X\setminus Z\xrightarrow{i_{U}}X
be the open complement of
Z\hookrightarrow X
. Then, the following sequence is exact

A_{j}(Z)\xrightarrow{i_{Z*}}A_{j}(X)\xrightarrow{i_{U}^{*}}A_{j}(U)\rightarrow 0\quad\forall j\geq 0.

Let
Y
be a smooth variety and
X\hookrightarrow Y
be a smooth subvariety with
d=\dim Y-\dim X
.
We denote
f:\tilde{Y}\rightarrow Y
as the blowup of
Y
along
X
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

where
\tilde{X}
is the exceptional locus of
f
.

Under the aforementioned hypothesis, we have explicit and linearly independent generators for
A^{*}(\tilde{Y})
in terms of
A^{*}(Y)
and
A^{*}(X)
from
[
4
, Proposition 0.1.3]
.
The intersection product on
A^{*}(\tilde{Y})
is described in
[
8
, Proposition 13.12]
. We state the results below.

Theorem 2.3
.

The Chow ring
A^{*}(\tilde{Y})
is generated by
f^{*}(A^{*}(Y))
and
j_{*}(A^{*}(\tilde{X}))
, that is,
classes pulled back from
Y
and classes supported on
\tilde{X}
. Let
\zeta:=-c_{1}(\mathcal{N}_{\tilde{X}/\tilde{Y}})=c_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{X}}(1))

be the relative hyperplane class of the projective bundle defined by
g
. The product satisfies

\displaystyle f^{*}\alpha\cdot f^{*}\beta=f^{*}(\alpha\cdot\beta)\quad\forall\alpha,\beta\in A^{*}(Y),

\displaystyle f^{*}\alpha.j_{*}\gamma=j_{*}(\gamma\cdot g^{*}i^{*}\alpha)\quad\forall\alpha\in A^{*}(Y),\gamma\in A^{*}(\tilde{X}),

\displaystyle j_{*}\gamma.j_{*}\delta=-j_{*}(\zeta.\gamma\cdot\delta)\quad\forall\gamma,\delta\in A^{*}(\tilde{X}).

Theorem 2.4
.

For
q\geq 1
, the Chow groups
A^{q}(\tilde{Y})
(resp.,
H^{q}(\tilde{Y})
) are given by the isomorphisms

A^{q}(\tilde{Y})\rightleftarrows A^{q}(Y)\oplus_{r=0}^{c}A^{q-1-r}({X})\quad\left(\text{resp., }H^{q}(\tilde{X})\rightleftarrows H^{q}(X)\oplus_{r=0}^{c}H^{q-2-2r}({X})\right)

where
c=d-2
.

•

The map
A^{q}(\tilde{Y})\rightarrow A^{q}(Y)\oplus_{r=0}^{c}A^{q-1-r}({X})
is given by

z\mapsto f^{*}f_{*}z+\oplus_{r=0}^{c}g_{*}(\gamma_{c-r}\cdot j^{*}z)

where
\gamma_{r}:=\zeta^{r}+\zeta^{r-1}g^{*}c_{1}(N)+\cdots+g^{*}c_{r}(N)\forall 0\leq r\leq c
and
N:=\mathcal{N}_{X/Y}
.

•

The map
A^{q}(Y)\oplus_{r=0}^{c}A^{q-1-r}({X})\rightarrow A^{q}(\tilde{Y})
is given by

x+\Sigma_{r=0}^{c}y_{r}\mapsto f^{*}x-j_{*}(\Sigma_{r=0}^{c}\zeta^{r}\cdot g^{*}y_{r}).

For a subvariety
V\hookrightarrow Y
, the class of its strict tranform
\tilde{V}\hookrightarrow\tilde{Y}
in
A^{*}(\tilde{Y})
is given by
[
16
, Theorem 6.7]
as stated below.

Theorem 2.5
.

Let
V
be a
k
-dimensional subvariety of
Y
, and let
\tilde{V}\hookrightarrow\tilde{Y}
be the strict transform of
V
, i.e. the blow-up of
V
along
V\cap X
. Then,

[\tilde{V}]=f^{*}[V]-j_{*}\{c(E)\cap g^{*}s(V\cap X,V)\}_{k}\in A_{k}(\tilde{Y})

where
E:=g^{*}N/\mathcal{N}_{\tilde{X}/\tilde{Y}}=g^{*}N/\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{X}}(-1)
and
N:=\mathcal{N}_{X/Y}
.

Corollary 1
.

The computations in the previous theorem get simplified when
\dim(V\cap X)
is close to the expected dimension. In particular,

•

If
\dim(V\cap X)=\dim V-d
, we have

f^{*}V=[\tilde{V}]

•

If
\dim(V\cap X)=\dim V-d+1
and equidimensional, we have

[\tilde{V}]=f^{*}V-j_{*}g^{*}(V\cap X).

The following remark shall be used extensively in the upcoming sections and can be found in
[
33
, Remark 2.2]
.

Remark 2
.

If
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a finite, flat map of degree
d
.
Then
f^{*}:A^{*}(Y)\hookrightarrow A^{*}(X)
is injective with
\dfrac{1}{d}f_{*}
as its one-sided inverse.
In particular,
A^{*}(X)=\mathbb{Q}\implies A^{*}(Y)=\mathbb{Q}
.

Remark 3
.

Using a Moving Lemma, one can relax the condition of flatness in Remark
2
to surjective for
X,Y
smooth and quasi-projective.

2.3.
The Two Stacks

There are two versions of the moduli functor for families of (semi)stable bundles on smooth curves.
Consequently, we get two different stacks
\mathscr{U}(r,d,g),\mathcal{U}(r,d,g)
for the moduli of (semi)stable bundles of rank
r
, degree
d
and genus
g
.
The categories
\mathscr{U}(r,d,g),\mathcal{U}(r,d,g)
consist of identical objects

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{S}}

which are families of fiberwise (semi)stable bundles of rank
r
and relative degree
d
over smooth curves
\mathscr{C}_{S}\rightarrow S
of genus
g
.

For both stacks, morphisms between two such families can be defined using the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

For
\mathscr{U}(r,d,g)
, the morphisms between objects shown above are given by a pair
(\pi_{f},\phi_{f})
where
\pi_{f}:\mathscr{C}_{S}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}_{T}
is such that square above is Cartesian and
\phi_{f}:\pi_{f}^{*}\mathcal{E}_{T}\simeq\mathcal{E}_{S}
is an isomorphism.

For
\mathcal{U}(r,d,g)
, a morphism consists of the map
\pi_{f}
where
\pi_{f}:\mathscr{C}_{S}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}_{T}
is such that square above is Cartesian and there exists
\phi_{f}:\pi_{f}^{*}\mathcal{E}_{T}\simeq\mathcal{E}_{S}\otimes\pi_{S}^{*}L
for some line bundle
L\in Pic(S)
.

In order to motivate the two versions of the moduli functor, we state a generalized version of
[
30
, Lemma 5.10]
below.

Theorem 2.6
.

Let
\mathscr{C}\xrightarrow{\pi_{S}}S
be a family of smooth curves over a (quasi-projective) base
S
and
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2}
be families of stable bundles over
\pi_{S}
. We have
\mathcal{E}_{1}|_{\mathscr{C}_{s}}\simeq\mathcal{E}_{2}|_{\mathscr{C}_{s}}\forall s\in S
if and only if
\exists L\in Pic(S)
such that
\mathcal{E}_{1}\simeq\mathcal{E}_{2}\otimes\pi_{S}^{*}L
.

Proof.

The fiberwise isomorphism over
S
follows immediately from the existence of
L
. To show the converse, we prove that fiberwise isomorphism implies that the claimed isomorphism is satisfied by

L=\pi_{S*}{\mathscr{H}om}(\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{E}_{1})

Cohomology and Base-Change imply that
L
is a line bundle since
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2}
are families of stable bundles. The induced morphism

\mathcal{E}_{2}\otimes\pi_{S}^{*}L=\mathcal{E}_{2}\otimes\pi_{S}^{*}\pi_{S*}\mathscr{H}om(\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{E}_{2})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{2}\otimes\mathscr{H}om(\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{E}_{1})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{1}

is the claimed isomorphism.

To show that the induced morphism is indeed an isomorphism, it is enough to show that it is non-zero since fibers of
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2}
are stable. This is equivalent to showing that the canonical map

\pi_{S}^{*}\pi_{S*}\mathscr{H}om(\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{E}_{2})\rightarrow\mathscr{H}om(\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{E}_{1})

is non-zero, which can be verified by restricting the morphism over any fiber of
\pi_{S}
.
∎

Remark 4
.

It has been established in
[
14
, Theorem 1.2.2]
that
\mathscr{U}(r,d,g),\mathcal{U}(r,d,g)
are irreducible, smooth Artin stacks of finite type.

Theorem 2.7
.

For
d
odd, we have

A^{*}(U(2,d,2))\simeq A^{*}(\mathcal{U}(2,d,2))

However, for
d
even, we have the following relations between the Chow rings of
\mathcal{U}(2,d,2)
and
U(2,d,2)

A^{*}(U(2,d,2))\hookrightarrow A^{*}(\mathcal{U}(2,d,2)),A^{*}(U^{s}(2,d,2))\simeq A^{*}(\mathcal{U}^{s}(2,d,2))

where
U^{s}(2,d,2)
is the coarse moduli space of
\mathcal{U}^{s}(2,d,2)
, the open substack of
\mathcal{U}(2,d,2)
determined by stable bundles.

Proof.

The good moduli space of both
\mathscr{U}(r,d,g),\mathcal{U}(r,d,g)
is
U(r,d,g)
, and the coarse moduli space of
\mathcal{U}^{s}(r,d,g)
is
U^{s}(r,d,g)
by
[
1
, Theorem 11.4, Theorem 13.6]
and
[
17
, §C.2]
.

For any value of
d
, the isomorphism

A^{*}(U^{s}(2,d,2))\simeq A^{*}(\mathcal{U}^{s}(2,d,2))

follows from
[
39
, Proposition 6.1]
and
[
31
, Remark 8.3.4]
since
\mathcal{U}^{s}(2,d,2)
is a Deligne-Mumford stack.

For
d
even, the inclusion

A^{*}(U(2,d,2))\hookrightarrow A^{*}(\mathcal{U}(2,d,2))

is established in
[
12
, Theorem 1.1, Theorem 1.7]
.
∎

2.4.
Tautological Classes

For the case
r=1
or in codimension
1
, tautological classes on the stacks
\mathscr{U}{(r,d,g)},\mathcal{U}{(r,d,g)}
have been defined in many papers including

[
14
]
,
[
24
]
, and
[
26
]
. We present some results and definitions from
[
14
]
and
[
24
]
below, along with some original statements, and include proofs wherever necessary.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{d}
be the Poincaré bundle for the family

\mathscr{C}_{2}\times_{\mathcal{M}_{2}}\mathscr{U}(2,d,2)\xrightarrow{\pi_{\mathscr{U}}}\mathscr{U}(2,d,2)
where
\mathscr{C}_{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{2}
is the universal curve.

We generalize the
\kappa
-classes defined in
[
24
]
for
\mathscr{U}(1,d,g)|_{\mathcal{H}_{g}}
and the classical
\kappa
-classes on
\mathcal{M}_{g}
as defined in
[
27
]
in the definition below.

Definition 1
.

The twisted-kappa classes generate the tautological ring and are defined as follows:

\kappa_{a,b,c}:=\pi_{\mathscr{U}*}(K_{\pi_{\mathscr{U}}}^{a+1}c_{1}(\mathcal{E}_{d})^{b}c_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{d})^{c})\in A^{a+b+2c}(\mathscr{U}(2,d,2))\text{ for }a\geq-1,b,c\geq 0.

Furthermore, the classes

\Lambda(m,n,l):=d_{\pi_{\mathscr{U}}}(K_{\pi_{\mathscr{U}}}^{\otimes m}\otimes{\det\mathcal{E}_{d}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{d}^{\otimes l})\in Pic(\mathscr{U}(2,d,2))

as defined in
[
14
]
, can easily be expressed in terms of the
\kappa
classes by a straightforward application of the Grothendieck-Riemann-Roch Theorem.

We have the following description of the Picard groups of
\mathscr{U}(2,d,2)
(and
\mathscr{U}(r,d,g)
in general for
r\geq 1,g\geq 2
) in terms of the
\Lambda
-classes in
[
14
, Theorem A, A.1, A.2, B]
and
[
26
, Theorem A, B]
.

Theorem 2.8
.

The Picard groups of
\mathscr{U}(2,d,2)
are generated by
\Lambda(1,0,0),\Lambda(1,1,0),\Lambda(0,1,0),\Lambda(0,0,1)
and the Picard groups of
\mathcal{U}(2,d,2)
are generated by
\Lambda(1,0,0),\Xi,\Theta
with the unique relation

\Lambda(1,0,0)^{10}=\mathcal{O}

where

\Xi=\Lambda(0,1,0)^{{(d+1)}/{n_{2,d-1}}}\otimes\Lambda(1,1,0)^{-{(d-1)}/{n_{2,d-1}}},\Theta=\Lambda(0,0,1)^{2n_{2,d-1}/{n_{2,d}}}\otimes\Lambda(0,1,0)^{\alpha}\otimes\Lambda(1,1,0)^{\beta}

and
\alpha,\beta
are an integral solution to
\alpha(d-1)+\beta(d+1)=-\dfrac{n_{2,d-1}(d-2)}{n_{2,d}},n_{2,m}=gcd(m,2)\forall m\in\mathbb{Z}
.

The Chow rings of
\mathscr{J}_{d,g}:=\mathscr{U}(1,d,g)|_{\mathcal{H}_{g}},J_{d,g};=\mathcal{U}(1,d,g)|_{\mathcal{H}_{g}}
, as stated below, were calculated in
[
24
, Theorem 1.1]
.

Theorem 2.9
.

The Chow ring of
\mathscr{J}_{d,g}
is given by

A^{*}(\mathscr{J}_{d,g})=\dfrac{\mathbb{Q}[\kappa_{0,1,0},\kappa_{-1,2,0}]}{<(d\kappa_{0,1,0}-(g-1)\kappa_{-1,2,0})^{g+1}>}\quad\forall g\geq 2,d\in\mathbb{Z}.

Furthermore, the Chow ring of its rigidification
J_{g}^{d}
is a subring of
A^{*}(\mathscr{J}_{d,g})
given by

A^{*}(J_{g}^{d})=\dfrac{\mathbb{Q}[u]}{<u^{g+1}>}\hookrightarrow A^{*}(\mathscr{J}_{d,g});u\mapsto d\kappa_{0,1,0}-(g-1)\kappa_{-1,2,0}.

The following result about
\nu_{r,d}^{*}
shall prove useful in §
5
.

Theorem 2.10
.

The pullback map
\nu_{r,d}^{*}:A^{*}(\mathcal{U}(r,d,g))\rightarrow A^{*}(\mathscr{U}(r,d,g))
is injective. That is,
\nu_{r,d}^{*}
induces an isomorphism onto its image, given by
A^{*}(\mathscr{U}(r,d,g))^{\mathcal{B}\mathbb{G}_{m}}
.

Proof.

We start by showig that
\nu_{r,d}^{*}
is injective.
Assuming the contrary, let
Z\in ker(\nu_{r,d}^{*})\setminus 0
be a non-trivial cycle in its kernel.
Thus,
\exists t:S\rightarrow\mathcal{U}(r,d,g)
for a scheme
S
such that
t^{*}Z\neq 0
.

The morphism
t
is given by a family

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{S}}

defined upto twisting by an element in
\pi_{S}^{*}Pic(S)
.

For any choice of such a family, we can define a lift
t_{\mathcal{E}_{S}}:S\rightarrow\mathscr{U}(r,d,g)
of
t
via
\nu_{r,d}
.

Consequently, we have the desired contradiction by

0=t_{\mathcal{E}_{S}}^{*}\nu_{r,d}^{*}Z=t^{*}Z\neq 0.

It remains to show that

A^{*}(\mathscr{U}(r,d,g))^{\mathcal{B}\mathbb{G}_{m}}=Im(\nu_{r,d}^{*}).

Containment of
Im(\nu_{r,d}^{*})
follows as an immediate consequence of the isomorphism
\mathscr{U}(r,d,g)/\mathcal{B}\mathbb{G}_{m}\simeq\mathscr{U}(r,d,g)\hbox{\hskip 5.00002pt\hskip-5.00002pt\hbox{/}\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{7.2pt}{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\rule{3.0pt}{0.4pt}\hss}}}}\kern-2.20001pt\hbox{\hbox{/}\hskip-5.00002pt\raisebox{-2.1pt}{\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\rule{3.0pt}{0.4pt}\kern-0.1pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 5.00002pt}\mathbb{G}_{m}=\mathcal{U}(r,d,g)
by
[
17
, §C.2]
.

To show the converse, let
W\in A^{*}(\mathscr{U}(r,d,g))^{\mathcal{B}\mathbb{G}_{m}}
be a cycle. We show that
W\in Im(\nu_{r,d}^{*})
.

In order to do that, it is enough to define cycles
f^{*}W\in A^{*}(S)
as pullbacks over any morphism
f:S\rightarrow\mathcal{U}(r,d,g)
such that these pullbacks satisfy the necessary functorial properties.

As established above, any such morphism
f:S\rightarrow\mathcal{U}(r,d,g)
has a lift
f_{\mathcal{E}_{S}}:S\rightarrow\mathscr{U}(r,d,g)
. We define
f^{*}W:=f_{\mathcal{E}_{S}}^{*}W\in A^{*}(S)
.

We show that this definition of
f^{*}W
is

(1)

Independent of the choice of lift,
f_{\mathcal{E}_{S}}
,

(2)

Satisfies the necessary functorial properties.

We have
(1)\implies(2)
since
W\in A^{*}(\mathscr{U}(r,d,g))
and thus, the pullbacks
f_{\mathcal{E}_{S}}^{*}W
satisfy the necessary functorial properties.

In order to show independence of chosen lifts, we use the
\mathcal{B}\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action on
\mathscr{U}(r,d,g)
.
The orbit of an object
(\mathcal{E}_{S}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}_{S}\xrightarrow{\pi_{S}}S)
is given by
\{(\mathcal{E}_{S}\otimes\pi_{S}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{S}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}_{S}\xrightarrow{\pi_{S}}S)|\mathcal{L}_{S}\in Pic(S)\}
.
Since
W\in A^{*}(\mathscr{U}(r,d,g))^{\mathcal{B}\mathbb{G}_{m}}
, we have

f_{\mathcal{E}_{S}}^{*}W=f_{\mathcal{E}_{S}\otimes\pi_{S}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{S}}^{*}W\quad\forall\mathcal{L}_{S}\in Pic(S).

Now,
f_{\mathcal{E}_{S}}:S\rightarrow\mathscr{U}(r,d,g)
is a lift of
f:S\rightarrow\mathcal{U}(r,d,g)
if and only if we have the same for
f_{\mathcal{E}_{S}\otimes\pi_{S}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{S}}\forall\mathcal{L}_{S}\in Pic(S)
, establishing
(1)
.
∎

3.
Even degree

We consider the moduli space of semistable rank
2
vector bundles with determinant of relative degree
2d
for the family
\pi
and call it
\tilde{U}(2,2d,2)
.
Let
|2\Theta_{*}|_{\pi}:=\mathbb{P}_{M_{*}}(\pi_{*}L_{\Theta_{*}}^{2})
be the relative linear system.
We have a unique component of the relative Picard scheme of
\pi
, for every relative degree
d\in\mathbb{Z}
, rigified along the section
\sigma_{1}
in the sense of
[
22
, Definition 2.8]
.
We shall refer to this component as
J_{*}(d)
from hereon.
We represent the
2
-torsion points in the abelian scheme
J_{*}(0)\rightarrow M_{*}
by
\Gamma_{*}
.We have the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mid 2\Theta_{*}\mid_{\pi}\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(d)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{U}(2,2d,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U(2,2d,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mid 2\Theta_{*}\mid_{\pi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{*}(d)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Theta_{*}:=)\quad\mathscr{C}_{*}\hookrightarrow J_{*}(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces/\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces/S_{6}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{1}\,\cdots\,\sigma_{6}}

The induced morphism
\Gamma_{*}\rightarrow M_{*}
, is apriori, a degree
16
cover.
The sections
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq 6}
define a trivialization of this cover since
2
-torsion points of a genus
2
curve are given by the pairwise differences of its Weierstrass points.

Consequently,
\Gamma_{*}\simeq\mu_{2}^{\oplus 4}\times M_{*}
, where we identify
\mu_{2}^{\oplus 4}
with its associated group variety.
The étale equivalence relation defined using twisting by the
2
-torsion points
\Gamma_{*}\rightarrow M_{*}
is therefore equivalent to the finite-group action of
\mu_{2}^{\oplus 4}
on
|2\Theta_{*}|_{\pi}\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(2d)
and is uniquely defined by the isomorphism
\Gamma_{*}\simeq\mu_{2}^{\oplus 4}\times M_{*}
.

It is worth noting that there is no canonical choice for the isomorphism
\Gamma_{*}\simeq\mu_{2}^{\oplus 4}\times M_{*}
. We shall fix one such isomorphism and refer to the corresponding group action as
\Gamma
from hereon.

Remark 5
.

A point worth noting is that the splitting of
\Gamma_{*}
as
\mu_{2}^{\oplus 4}\times M_{*}
is not naturally defined. It is obtained using the fact that
\Gamma_{*}\hookrightarrow J_{*}(0)
is a degree
16
cover of
M_{*}
,
with
16
mutually disjoint sections given by
\{\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{C}_{*}}(\sigma_{i}-\sigma_{j})\}_{1\leq j<i\leq 6}
and the identity section of
J_{*}(0)\rightarrow M_{*}
.

For the following remark and thereafter, we denote the universal rigidified Picard stack of degree
d
over smooth genus
g
curves by
J^{d}_{g}
.

Remark 6
.

The
S_{6}
-action on
\mathscr{C}_{*}
, equivariance of
\pi
and universal property of
J_{*}(d)
induce an
S_{6}
-action on
J_{*}(d)
for all
d\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Furthermore, the map
J_{*}(1)\rightarrow J_{2}^{1}
is invariant under this action.

It is worth noting that the induced map
J_{*}(d)/S_{6}\rightarrow J^{d}_{2}
is finite and generically a double cover due to the involution on
\mathscr{C}_{*}
.

The natural embedding
\mathscr{C}_{*}\hookrightarrow J_{*}(d)
for
d
odd is invariant under this action and so is the image of the map
\mathscr{C}_{*}\rightarrow J_{*}(2)
given by the divisor of double points.
In fact, they are pullbacks of cycles in
J_{2}^{d}
under the map
J_{*}(d)\rightarrow J_{2}^{d}
.

Furthermore, the
S_{6}
-action on
J_{*}(1)
further induces an action on
L_{\Theta_{*}}^{2}
and consequently on
|2\Theta_{*}|_{\pi}
.

3.1.
The Universal Extension Space

We begin by extending the classical
[
28
, Theorem 3]
to the family
\pi
and rephrasing the theorem in terms of the universal extension space as defined in
[
34
]
.

In order to do that, we need to restate some definitions from
[
28
]
in terms of the family
\pi
using the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{*}(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{*}(0)\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{*}(0)\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{*}(1),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{*}}

where
\tau:=(\pi_{1}-\pi_{2},\pi_{2})
is defined using the canonical embedding
\Theta_{*}:\mathscr{C}_{*}\hookrightarrow J_{*}(1)
,

\pi_{J}
is the projection of
\mathscr{C}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(1)
onto
J_{*}(1)
,
\sigma:J_{*}(0)\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(1)\rightarrow J_{*}(0)\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(1)
is the pullback of
[-1]:J_{*}(0)\rightarrow J_{*}(0)
to
J_{*}(1)
,
and
\mu
is the twisting morphism.

Let
T:=Im(\tau)
and
L_{T}
denote the line bundle associated to
T
on
J_{*}(0)\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(1)
, and let
\xi_{1}
be the Poincaré bundle on
\mathscr{C}_{J}:=\mathscr{C}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(1)
rigidified along the section
\pi^{*}\sigma_{1}:J_{*}(1)\rightarrow\mathscr{C}_{J}
.

Lemma 3.1
.

There is a line bundle
L_{J}
on
J_{*}(1)
such that
\tau^{*}\sigma^{*}L_{T}=\xi_{1}^{\otimes 2}\otimes\pi_{J}^{*}L_{J}
with
c_{1}(L_{J})=4\Theta_{*}
.

Proof.

Existence of
L_{J}
follows from the observation that
\tau^{*}\sigma^{*}L_{T},\xi_{1}^{\otimes 2}
have identical restrictions on fibers over
J_{*}(1)
by
[
28
, Lemma 6.4]
.
To show
c_{1}(L_{J})=4\Theta_{*}
, we calculate
c_{1}(\tau^{*}\sigma^{*}L_{T}|_{\sigma_{1}\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(1)})\in A^{1}(J_{*}(1))
.
We have

\tau^{*}\sigma^{*}L_{T}|_{\sigma_{1}\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(1)}=[2]^{*}\Theta_{*}\implies c_{1}(\tau^{*}\sigma^{*}L_{T}|_{\sigma_{1}\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(1)})=4\Theta_{*}\in A^{1}(J_{*}(1))

finishing the proof.
∎

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
\tilde{U}(2,\mathcal{O},2)
be the moduli space of semistable rank
2
bundles with trivial relative determinant for the family
\pi
, and let

\mathcal{E}:=R^{1}\pi_{J*}\xi_{1}^{-\otimes 2}
be the universal extension bundle. We put
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}):=Proj_{J_{*}(1)}(\operatorname{Sym}\mathcal{E}^{\vee})
to be its projectivization, and put
JU_{*}:=J_{*}(1)\times_{M_{*}}\tilde{U}(2,\mathcal{O},2)
to get the diagram:

(1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces JU_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{U}(2,\mathcal{O},2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mid 2\Theta_{*}\mid_{\pi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}_{J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{*}(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

where the morphisms
s,f,D
are such that their restrictions to fibers over
M_{*}
coincide with
[
28
, Theorem 2]
. Moreover,
D
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

We start with constructing each of these morphisms. We have the Poincaré bundle
\mathcal{P}
on
\mathscr{C}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})
given by
[
34
, Remark 1]
applied to

\xi_{1}^{\otimes 2}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(1).

The bundle
\mathcal{P}\otimes(1\times p_{J})^{*}\xi_{1}^{-1}
defines the map
s:\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\tilde{U}(2,\mathcal{O},2)
.
Put
\mathcal{F}:=\pi_{J*}(\tau^{*}L_{T}\otimes\tau^{*}\sigma^{*}L_{T})\otimes L_{\Theta_{*}}^{-2}
. We use the following exact sequence in
[
28
, Proposition 6.1]
to define
f,D
:

0\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{\vee}\rightarrow\pi^{*}\pi_{*}L_{\Theta_{*}}^{2}\rightarrow L_{\Theta_{*}}^{2}\rightarrow 0.

Using Lemma
3.1
, we have

\mathcal{F}^{\vee}\simeq L_{\Theta_{*}}^{2}\otimes R^{1}\pi_{J*}(\tau^{*}\sigma^{*}L_{T})^{\vee}\simeq L_{\Theta_{*}}^{2}\otimes L_{J}^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{E}.

The injection defines an embedding
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})\simeq\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}^{\vee})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}_{J_{*}(1)}(\pi^{*}\pi_{*}L_{\Theta_{*}}^{2})=|2\Theta_{*}|_{\pi}\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(1)
, which in turn defines
f
.

The image of the induced morphism
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})\hookrightarrow JU_{*}
is a Cartier divisor, let
\mathscr{L}:=\mathcal{O}_{JU_{*}}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}))=\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})/JU_{*}}^{\vee}
be the line bundle determined by this divisor.
From the proof of
[
28
, Theorem 2]
, the restrictions of
\mathscr{L}\otimes p_{J}^{*}L_{\Theta_{*}}^{-2}
to the fibers of
p_{U}
are trivial.
So,
\mathscr{L}\otimes p_{J}^{*}L_{\Theta_{*}}^{-2}\simeq p_{U}^{*}L
for some
L\in Pic(\tilde{U}(2,\mathcal{O},2))
.
We will show that there is an injection
L^{\vee}\hookrightarrow\lambda^{*}(\pi_{*}L_{\Theta_{*}}^{2})
and use this injection to define
D
.

We have the inclusion
\mathcal{O}_{JU_{*}}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{L}
induced by the inclusion
\mathcal{I}_{P(\mathcal{E})/JU_{*}}\subset\mathcal{O}
.
Pushing forward this injection via
p_{U}
, we get
\mathcal{O}\hookrightarrow p_{U*}\mathcal{L}\simeq L\otimes p_{U*}p_{J}^{*}L_{\Theta_{*}}^{2}
.
Twisting this by
L^{\vee}
, we get the desired injection
L^{\vee}\hookrightarrow p_{U*}p_{J}^{*}L_{\Theta_{*}}^{2}\simeq\lambda^{*}(\pi_{*}L_{\Theta_{*}}^{2})
.

By
[
28
, Theorem 2]
, we know that
D
is a bijection. Therefore,
D
must be an isomorphism by Theorem
2.1
.
∎

Remark 7
.

A version of the above theorem can be found in
[
37
]
, in arbitrary characteristic, without proof.
We’ve added a proof here for completeness and in order to define the explicit construction of the morphisms for the family
\pi
.

Using
A^{*}(M_{*})\simeq\mathbb{Q}
, we have
A^{*}(|2\Theta_{*}|_{\pi})\simeq\mathbb{Q}[\zeta]/(\zeta^{4})
where
\zeta
is the relative hyperplane divisor in
|2\Theta_{*}|_{\pi}
.

Results on the Picard group of
U(2,\mathcal{O},2)
in
[
14
]
show that
\zeta
can be identified with the class of the generalized
\Theta
-divisor on fibers over
M_{*}
.
Since
Pic(M_{*})=0
,
[\Theta_{gen}]=\zeta
in
A^{1}(\tilde{U}(2,\mathcal{O},2))
where
\Theta_{gen}\hookrightarrow\tilde{U}(2,\mathcal{O},2)
is the pullback of the universal generalized
\Theta
-divisor,
\Theta_{2,gen}
, via the map
\tilde{U}(2,\mathcal{O},2)\xrightarrow{\otimes\mathcal{O}(\sigma_{1})}\tilde{U}(2,2,2)
.

We provide another proof below using
[
28
, Theorem 2]
.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
\Theta_{gen}^{K}
be the universal generalized
\Theta
-divisor restrcited to
\tilde{U}(2,\mathcal{K}_{\pi},2)\hookrightarrow\tilde{U}(2,2,2)
.
The image of
\Theta_{gen}^{K}
is a hyperplane bundle under the morphism
D\otimes\mathcal{O}(-\sigma_{1}):\tilde{U}(2,\mathcal{K}_{\pi},2)\xrightarrow{\otimes\mathcal{O}(-\sigma_{1})}\tilde{U}(2,\mathcal{O},2)\rightarrow|2\Theta_{*}|_{\pi}
.

Proof.

We have
[E]\in\Theta_{gen}^{K}
if and only if
h^{0}(E)>0
for
[E]\in U(2,\mathcal{K}_{\pi},2)
. Let
\Theta_{gen}^{K}\otimes\mathcal{O}(-\sigma_{1})
be the divisor in
\tilde{U}(2,\mathcal{O},2)
obtained by twisting bundles in the locus
\Theta_{gen}^{K}
by
\mathcal{O}(-\sigma_{1})
.
It is easy to see that
\Theta_{gen}=\Theta_{gen}^{K}\otimes\mathcal{O}(-\sigma_{1})
. We show that
\Theta_{gen}^{K}\otimes\mathcal{O}(-\sigma_{1})
maps to a hyperplane on every fibre over
M_{*}
under
D
.

Let
p\in M_{*}
be a point and
[W]\in U(2,\mathcal{O},2)_{p}
. For
[W]\in\Theta_{gen}^{K}\otimes\mathcal{O}(-\sigma_{1})
, we must have

h^{0}(\mathscr{C}_{p},W\otimes\mathcal{O}(\sigma_{1})_{p})>0
.
That is,

[W]\in\Theta_{gen}^{K}\otimes\mathcal{O}(-\sigma_{1})\iff(\sigma_{1})_{p}\in C_{W}.

The locus of divisors in
|2\Theta_{\mathscr{C}_{p}}|
that contain the point
(\sigma_{1})_{p}
is a hyperplane. Therefore,
D_{p}
maps
\Theta_{gen,p}^{K}\otimes\mathcal{O}(-\sigma_{1})_{p}
onto a hyperplane in
|2\Theta_{\mathscr{C}_{p}}|
.

Using
Pic(M_{*})=0
, we can conclude that
[\Theta_{gen}\otimes\mathcal{O}(\sigma_{1})]=\zeta
in
A^{1}(\tilde{U}(2,\mathcal{O},2))
.
∎

3.2.
Chow rings of
J_{*}(d)

For arbitrary integers
d,d^{\prime}
, twisting by
(d^{\prime}-d)\sigma_{1}
induces the isomorphisms
\tau_{d,d^{\prime}}:J_{*}(d)\xrightarrow{\simeq}J_{*}(d^{\prime})
.

We calculate
A^{*}(J_{*}(2))
and using
\tau_{d,2}
, we would then have
A^{*}(J_{*}(d))
for any
d\in\mathbb{Z}
. Furthermore, we can define an Abelian scheme structure on
J_{*}(2)
over
M_{*}
using the map on
J_{*}(0)
and
\tau_{2,0}
.
We shall provide an explicit stratification to get generators for every codimension using the following lemma from
[
38
, Lemma 1.6]
:

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
C
be a curve of genus
g
. Let
n_{1},\cdots,n_{g}\in\mathbb{Z}
be non-zero integers such that
n_{1}+\cdots+n_{g}=d
, then the map
a_{n_{1},\cdots,n_{g}}:C^{g}\rightarrow Pic^{d}(C)
given by
(x_{1},\cdots,x_{g})\mapsto n_{1}x_{1}+\cdots+n_{g}x_{g}
is surjective.

We need to determine
A^{*}(\mathscr{C}_{*})
to obtain generators for the different strata in our stratification.

3.2.1.
\mathbf{A^{*}(\mathbf{\mathscr{C}}_{*})}

Let
\mathcal{W}_{*}:=\coprod_{1\leq j\leq 6}\sigma_{j}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}_{*}
be the locus of Weierstrass points.

We have
2[\sigma_{i}]_{t}=2[\sigma_{j}]_{t}\forall 1\leq i,j\leq 6,\forall t\in M_{*}
. That is, the restriction of
2([\sigma_{i}]-[\sigma_{j}])
to any fiber over
M_{*}
is trivial and, therefore,
2([\sigma_{i}]-[\sigma_{j}])=\pi^{*}L_{ij}
for some
L_{ij}\in Pic(M_{*})
.
Since
M_{*}\subset\mathbb{A}^{3}
, we have that

[\sigma_{i}]=[\sigma_{j}]\in A^{1}(\mathscr{C}_{*})\forall 1\leq i,j\leq 6.

Additionally, there are no non-trivial cycles inside any of the
\sigma_{i}
.

Using the stratification
\mathscr{C}_{*}=(\mathscr{C}_{*}\setminus\mathcal{W}_{*})\coprod_{i}\sigma_{i}
, we get the excision sequence

\mathbb{Q}.\mathcal{K}\rightarrow A^{*}(\mathscr{C}_{*})\rightarrow A^{*}(\mathscr{C}_{*}\setminus\mathcal{W}_{*})\rightarrow 0

where
\mathcal{K}:=[K_{\mathscr{C}_{*}/M_{*}}]=\dfrac{1}{3}\Sigma_{1\leq i\leq 6}[\sigma_{i}]=2[\sigma_{i}]\forall 1\leq i\leq 6
.

Lemma 3.3
.

The Chow ring of
\mathscr{C}_{*}
is generated by pushforward of cycles in
\mathcal{W}_{*}
. That is,

A^{*}(\mathscr{C}_{*}\setminus\mathcal{W}_{*})=\mathbb{Q}.

Proof.

Let
(C,x)
be a pointed curve of genus
2
such that
x
is not a Weierstrass point of
C
. The map
\phi_{x}
given by

C\xrightarrow[|4x|]{}\mathbb{P}^{2}

maps
C
into the plane as a degree
4
curve with a single point of singularity that is either nodal or cuspidal, and a
4
-flex at the image of
x
. Let
\tilde{y}
be the conjugate of
y
under the hyperelliptic involution for any
y\in C
.

To rigidify this map, let’s fix the node to be
N=[1:0:0]
, the image of the point
x
to be
M=[0:1:0]
, and the inflection line to be
l:x_{1}=0
where the coordinates are given by
[x_{1}:x_{2}:x_{3}]
.

The equation of the image
\phi_{x}(C)\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
then has the form
\lambda_{1}x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}+\lambda_{2}x_{1}^{2}x_{2}x_{3}+\lambda_{3}x_{3}^{2}x_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{4}x_{1}x_{2}^{3}+\lambda_{5}x_{1}x_{2}^{2}x_{3}+\lambda_{6}x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}^{2}+\lambda_{7}x_{1}x_{3}^{3}+\lambda_{8}x_{3}^{4}
for some
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
.

The projective transformations fixing
N,\phi_{x}(x),l
are given by the group
\left\{\left(\begin{matrix}a&0&0\\
0&b&c\\
0&0&d\end{matrix}\right)\right\}\subset GL_{3}(\mathbb{C})
as described in
[
7
, §2]
.

Since
N
is the node, for any
y\in C
, the points
\{\phi_{x}(y),\phi_{x}(\tilde{y}),N\}\subset\mathbb{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
are collinear.
Intersection with the line
x_{3}=0
is given by the ideal
(x_{3},x_{2}^{2}x_{1}(\lambda_{1}x_{1}+\lambda_{4}x_{2}))
.
Since
x
is not a Weierstrass point, we have
\lambda_{4}\neq 0
.
Furthermore,
\lambda_{8}\neq 0
since the image of
\phi_{x}
is integral.

The transformation
x_{1}\mapsto\lambda_{4}x_{1},x_{2}\mapsto x_{2},x_{3}\mapsto\lambda_{4}x_{3}+\dfrac{1}{3}\lambda_{5}x_{2}
is an element of this group.
An application of this kills the coefficient of
x_{1}x_{2}^{2}x_{3}
.
Additionally, we can normalize
\lambda_{4},\lambda_{8}
to
1
.

The equation of
Im(\phi_{x})
then has the form
E_{C,x}:\alpha_{1}x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}+\alpha_{2}x_{1}^{2}x_{2}x_{3}+\alpha_{3}x_{3}^{2}x_{1}^{2}+x_{1}x_{2}^{3}+\alpha_{4}x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}^{2}+\alpha_{5}x_{1}x_{3}^{3}+x_{3}^{4}
.

To find images of Weierstrass points on
E_{C,x}
, we intersect lines passing through
N
. Equations of these lines are given by
\{x_{2}-cx_{3}=0\mid c\in\mathbb{C}\}
since the line
x_{3}=0
does not correspond to Weierstrass points.

Intersection with the line
x_{2}-cx_{3}=0
is given by the ideal
(x_{2}-cx_{3},x_{3}^{2}((c^{2}\alpha_{1}+c\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3})x_{1}^{2}+x_{3}^{2}+x_{1}x_{3}(\alpha_{5}+c\alpha_{4}+c^{3})))
. To get a Weierstrass point, we must have

(2)

(\alpha_{5}+c\alpha_{4}+c^{3})^{2}=4(c^{2}\alpha_{1}+c\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3})

The space of ordered roots to this polynomial is the set of points in
\mathbb{A}^{6}
, with mutually distinct co-ordinates adding up to
0
.

Let
f:\mathbb{A}^{5}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{6}
be the morphism given by
(\beta_{i})\mapsto(\beta_{i},-\Sigma_{i}\beta_{i})
and
U\subset\mathbb{A}^{6}
be the set of points with mutually distinct co-ordinates.

Let
\tilde{U}=f^{-1}(U)
. We have the morphism
f:\tilde{U}\rightarrow U
and two points
p,q\in\tilde{U}
determine projectively related
E_{C,x}
if and only if
\exists\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
such that
p=\lambda.q
.
The induced
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on the coefficients
(\alpha_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 5}\in\mathbb{A}^{5}
is given by
\lambda.(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{5})=(\lambda^{4}\alpha_{1},\lambda^{5}\alpha_{2},\lambda^{6}.\alpha_{3},\lambda^{2}.\alpha_{4},\lambda^{3}.\alpha_{5})
.

Shrinking
\tilde{U}
if necessary, we have that the coordinates of any point in
\tilde{U}
are roots of some suitable polynomial as in (
2
) which determine the coefficients of some
E_{C,x}
. Let
E_{U}
be the associated family of plane curves given by the
\{\alpha_{i}\}
.

Let
\tau_{M}:=M\times\tilde{U},\tau_{N}:=N\times\tilde{U}
be the sections corresponding to the marked point and node respectively.
We consider the desingularization of
E_{U}
at the singularity
\tau_{N}
to obtain
bl:\mathscr{C}_{U}\rightarrow E_{U}
, let
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{U}
be the space obtained from the corresponding blowups of
\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\tilde{U}
.
We get the following diagram:

(3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{U}\hookleftarrow\mathscr{C}_{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\tilde{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{U}//\mathbb{C}^{*}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces bl}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N\times\tilde{U}(=:\tau_{N})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\tau_{1}}{,}\cdots{,}\overline{\tau_{6}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\tau}}

The map
\tilde{\pi}
defines a family of smooth genus
2
curves. For any
1\leq i\leq 6
, we have a family of lines parameterized over
\tilde{U}
given by
\tau_{i}(p)=Z(x_{2}-f(p)_{i}x_{3})\forall p\in\mathbb{A}^{5}
.

The section
\tau_{M}
lifts to
\mathscr{C}_{U}
since
\mathscr{C}_{U}\setminus bl^{-1}(\tau_{N})\simeq E_{U}\setminus\tau_{N}
.

For every
\tau_{i}
, the strict transform
\tilde{\tau_{i}}\hookrightarrow\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{U}
intersects
\mathscr{C}_{U}
on a locus that maps bijectively onto
\tilde{U}
via
\tilde{\pi}
.
Hence, the locus
(\tilde{\tau_{i}}\cap\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{U})_{red}
is isomorphic to
\tilde{U}
by Theorem
2.1
. Let the sections induced by these isomorphisms be
\overline{\tau_{i}}
.
The sections
\{\overline{\tau}_{i}\}
provide markings for the Weierstrass points in the family
\tilde{\pi}
.

By the universality of
M_{*}
, we have the induced morphism
t:\tilde{U}\rightarrow M_{*}
. Additionally,
t
must lift to a morphism of families

t_{U}:\mathscr{C}_{U}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}_{*}
such that the square below is Cartesian.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}_{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\pi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

The map
t_{U}\circ\tau_{M}
is preserved under the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action and thus factors via
\tilde{\tau}:\tilde{U}//\mathbb{C}^{*}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}_{*}
. The map
\tilde{\tau}
is a bijection between
\tilde{U}//\mathbb{C}^{*}
and
\mathscr{C}_{*}\setminus\mathcal{W}_{*}
. Hence, we have
\tilde{U}//\mathbb{C}^{*}\simeq\mathscr{C}_{*}\setminus\mathcal{W}_{*}
. Additionally,

\tilde{U}//\mathbb{C}^{*}\subset\mathbb{A}^{4}
is open since
\tilde{U}\subset\mathbb{A}^{5}
consists of points with distinct coordinates, establishing the claimed statement.
∎

Hence, we have that
A^{i}(\mathscr{C}_{*})=0\forall i>1
and
A^{1}(\mathscr{C}_{*})=\mathbb{Q}.\mathcal{K}
. Restriction of the divisor associated to
\mathcal{K}
, to a fiber of
\pi:\mathscr{C}_{*}\rightarrow M_{*}
yields
\mathcal{K}\neq 0
in
A^{1}(\mathscr{C}_{*})
. Using
\mathcal{K}=2\sigma_{j}\forall j
, we have
\dim A^{1}(\mathscr{C}_{*})=1
and the following corollary.

Corollary 2
.

The excision sequence for
\mathscr{C}_{*}
yields

A^{*}(\mathscr{C}_{*})=\mathbb{Q}[\mathcal{K}]/(\mathcal{K}^{2})=\mathbb{Q}[\sigma_{j}]/(\sigma_{j}^{2})\forall 1\leq j\leq 6.

3.2.2.
\mathbf{A^{*}(J_{*}(2))}

We have the morphism
\mathscr{C}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}\mathscr{C}_{*}\xrightarrow{\phi}J_{*}(2)
, given by the sequence of maps
\mathscr{C}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}\mathscr{C}_{*}\hookrightarrow J_{*}(1)\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(1)\rightarrow J_{*}(2)
where the last map is the twisting morphism.
Using Lemma
3.2
, we have that this map is surjective and proper over
M_{*}
. We also have the map
i_{[2]}:\mathscr{C}_{*}\rightarrow J_{*}(2)

given by the sequence of morphisms
\mathscr{C}_{*}\hookrightarrow J_{*}(1)\xrightarrow{\Delta}J_{*}(1)\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(1)\rightarrow J_{*}(2)
.

We make a similar argument as presented in Lemma
3.3
to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 3.4
.

The Chow ring of
J_{*}(2)
is generated by cycles in
Z_{\mathscr{C}},\phi(\mathcal{W}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}\mathscr{C}_{*})
where
Z_{\mathscr{C}}:=Im(i_{[2]})
. That is,

A^{*}(J_{*}(2)\setminus(Z_{\mathscr{C}}\cup\phi(\mathcal{W}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}\mathscr{C}_{*})))\simeq\mathbb{Q}.

Proof.

Let
(C,x,y)
be a curve with two distinct marked points such that
x
is not a Weierstrass point of
C
and
y\neq\tilde{x}
.
Using similar arguments as in Lemma
3.3
, the map given by

C\xrightarrow[\phi_{x,y}]{|3x+y|}\mathbb{P}^{2},

embeds
C
into the plane as a degree
4
curve with a single point of singularity that is either nodal or cuspidal. We rigidify this map by setting the node to be
N,\phi_{x,y}(x)=M
and
\phi_{x,y}(y)=P:=[0:0:1]
(the inflection line at
M
still has the same equation as in Lemma
3.3
).

The equation of
\phi_{x,y}(C)\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
then has the form
\lambda_{1}x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}+\lambda_{2}x_{1}^{2}x_{2}x_{3}+\lambda_{3}x_{3}^{2}x_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{4}x_{1}x_{2}^{3}+\lambda_{5}x_{1}x_{2}^{2}x_{3}+\lambda_{6}x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}^{2}+\lambda_{7}x_{1}x_{3}^{3}+\lambda_{8}x_{2}x_{3}^{3}
for some
\{\lambda_{i}|1\leq i\leq 8\}\subset\mathbb{F}
.

The projective transformations fixing
N,\phi_{x,y}(x),\phi_{x,y}(y)
are given by the group
\left\{\left(\begin{matrix}a&0&0\\
0&b&0\\
0&0&c\end{matrix}\right)\right\}\subset GL_{3}
as described in
[
7
, §2]
.

Since
N
is the singular point, we have
\{\phi_{x,y}(a),\phi_{x,y}(\tilde{a}),N\}
are collinear for any
a\in C
. Intersection with the line
x_{3}=0
is the scheme given by the ideal
(x_{3},x_{2}^{2}x_{1}(\lambda_{1}x_{1}+\lambda_{4}x_{2}))
.
Therefore,
\lambda_{4}\neq 0
since
x
is not a Weierstrass point, and
\lambda_{8}\neq 0
since
Im(\phi_{x,y})
is irreducible.

We normalize
\lambda_{4},\lambda_{8}=1
. The equation of
Im(\phi_{x})
then has the form
E_{C,x}:\alpha_{1}x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}+\alpha_{2}x_{1}^{2}x_{2}x_{3}+\alpha_{3}x_{3}^{2}x_{1}^{2}+x_{1}x_{2}^{3}+\alpha_{4}x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}^{2}+\alpha_{5}x_{1}x_{2}^{2}x_{3}+\alpha_{6}x_{1}x_{3}^{3}+x_{2}x_{3}^{3}
.

The intersection with the line
Z(x_{2}-cx_{3})
is given by the ideal
(x_{2}-cx_{3},x_{3}^{2}((c^{2}\alpha_{1}+c\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3})x_{1}^{2}+cx_{3}^{2}+x_{1}x_{3}(c^{2}\alpha_{5}+c\alpha_{4}+c^{3}+\alpha_{6})))
.
To get a Weierstrass point, we must have

(4)

(c^{2}\alpha_{5}+c\alpha_{4}+c^{3}+\alpha_{6})^{2}=4c(c^{2}\alpha_{1}+c\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}).

Let
U_{0}
be the (ordered)space of roots to all such polynomials in
\mathbb{A}^{6}
, with mutually distinct co-ordinates and
U^{\prime}=\{(p_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 6}|(p_{i}^{2})_{1\leq i\leq 6}\in U_{0}\}
.

Two points
p,q\in U_{0}
determine projectively related
E_{C,x,y}
if and only if
\exists\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
such that
p=\lambda.q
. The corresponding
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on
(\alpha_{i})
is given by

\lambda.(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{6})=(\lambda^{3}\alpha_{1},\lambda^{4}\alpha_{2},\lambda^{5}.\alpha_{3},\lambda^{2}.\alpha_{4},\lambda.\alpha_{5},\lambda^{3}.\alpha_{6}).

For a point
p=(p_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 6}\in{U}^{\prime}
, we have that
\{p_{i}^{2}\}
are roots of some
E_{C,x,y}
as described in (
4
).
We can recover the coefficients
\{\alpha_{i}\}
from
p
by setting
\alpha_{6}
to be
\Pi_{1\leq i\leq 6}p_{i}
.

Following the notation established in (
3
) along with the section
\tau_{P}:=P\times U^{\prime}
, and desingularizing
E_{U^{\prime}}
along
\tau_{N}
, we get a family
\mathscr{C}_{U^{\prime}}
of smooth genus
2
curves.
Let
\mathbb{P}_{U^{\prime}}
be the space obtained from the corresponding blowups of
\mathbb{P}^{2}\times U^{\prime}
.
We have the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{U^{\prime}}\hookleftarrow\mathscr{C}_{U^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{U^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{2}\times U^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}\mathscr{C}_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U^{\prime}}//\mathbb{C}^{*}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces bl}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N\times{U}^{\prime}(=:\tau_{N})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{M}{,}\tau_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\tau_{1}}{,}\cdots{,}\overline{\tau_{6}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\tau}}

Furthermore, we have lines parametrized over
{U}^{\prime}
given by
\tau_{i}(p)=Z(x_{2}-p_{i}^{2}x_{3})
.

The sections
\tau_{M},\tau_{P}
lift to
\mathscr{C}_{U^{\prime}}
since
\mathscr{C}_{U^{\prime}}\setminus bl^{-1}(\tau_{N})\simeq E_{U^{\prime}}\setminus\tau_{N}
.

For every
\tau_{i}
, the strict transform
\tilde{\tau_{i}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}_{U^{\prime}}
intersects
\mathscr{C}_{U^{\prime}}
so that the intersection maps bijectively onto
{U}^{\prime}
via
\tilde{\pi}
. Hence, the locus
(\tilde{\tau_{i}}\cap\mathbb{P}_{U^{\prime}})_{red}
is isomorphic to
{U}^{\prime}
by Theorem
2.1
. Let the sections induced by these isomorphisms be
\overline{\tau_{i}}
.

Hence, we have a morphism
{U}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{t}M_{*}
. The sections
\tau_{M},\tau_{P}
then define the maps
\sigma_{M},\sigma_{P}:{U}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{}\mathscr{C}_{*}
. These maps define
\tau:U^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}\mathscr{C}_{*}
which is invariant under the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action and thus factors via
\tilde{\tau}:{U}^{\prime}//\mathbb{C}^{*}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}\mathscr{C}_{*}
.

Let
\tilde{\Delta}
be the image of an embedding of
\mathscr{C}_{*}
into
\mathscr{C}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}\mathscr{C}_{*}
with projection onto first factor given by the identity map and projection onto second factor given by the hyperelliptic involution.

The map
\tilde{\tau}
is finite, surjective onto
\mathscr{C}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}\mathscr{C}_{*}\setminus(\Delta\cup\tilde{\Delta}\cup(\mathcal{W}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}\mathscr{C}_{*}))
.

Additionally,
{U}^{\prime}//\mathbb{C}^{*}\subset\mathbb{A}^{5}
since
{U}^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{A}^{6}
consists of points with distinct coordinates. Therefore,

A^{*}(\mathscr{C}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}\mathscr{C}_{*}\setminus(\Delta\cup\tilde{\Delta}\cup(\mathcal{W}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}\mathscr{C}_{*})))\simeq\mathbb{Q}

by Remark
2
. Consequently, we have

\phi^{-1}(Z_{\mathscr{C}}\cup\phi(\mathcal{W}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}\mathscr{C}_{*}))=\Delta\cup\tilde{\Delta}\cup(\mathcal{W}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}\mathscr{C}_{*})\cup(\mathscr{C}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}\mathcal{W}_{*})\implies\phi^{-1}(J_{*}(2)\setminus(Z_{\mathscr{C}}\cup\phi(\mathcal{W}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}\mathscr{C}_{*})))\\
\subset\mathscr{C}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}\mathscr{C}_{*}\setminus(\Delta\cup\tilde{\Delta}\cup(\mathcal{W}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}\mathscr{C}_{*}))\implies A^{*}(\phi^{-1}(J_{*}(2)\setminus(Z_{\mathscr{C}}\cup\phi(\mathcal{W}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}\mathscr{C}_{*}))))=\mathbb{Q}.

The restriction of
\phi
to
\phi^{-1}(J_{*}(2)\setminus(Z_{\mathscr{C}}\cup\phi(\mathcal{W}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}\mathscr{C}_{*})))\rightarrow J_{*}(2)\setminus(Z_{\mathscr{C}}\cup\phi(\mathcal{W}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}\mathscr{C}_{*}))
is a finite, surjective map between smooth varieties. The lemma now follows from Remark
2
.
∎

Theorem 3.3
.

The Chow ring of
J_{*}(d)
is given by

A^{*}(J_{*}(d))\simeq\mathbb{Q}[\Theta_{d}]/(\Theta_{d}^{3})

for all
d\in\mathbb{Z}
. Consequently, the Chow rings of
J_{2}^{d}
are given by

A^{*}(J_{2}^{1})\simeq\mathbb{Q}[\Theta]/(\Theta^{3}),A^{*}(J_{2}^{2})\simeq\mathbb{Q}[Z]/(Z^{3})

where
\Theta
is the theta-divisor in
J_{2}^{1}
and
Z\hookrightarrow J_{2}^{2}
is the image of
Z_{\mathscr{C}}
under the map
J_{*}(2)\rightarrow J_{2}^{2}
.

Proof.

The deleted locus from
J_{*}(2)
in Lemma
3.4
is

Z_{\mathscr{C}}\cup\phi(\mathcal{W}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}\mathscr{C}_{*})=i_{[2]}(\mathscr{C}_{*})\cup_{i}\phi(\sigma_{i}\times_{M_{*}}\mathscr{C}_{*})=i_{[2]}(\mathscr{C}_{*})\cup_{j}\phi_{j}(\mathscr{C})

where
\phi_{j}:=\mathscr{C}_{*}\simeq\sigma_{j}\times_{M_{*}}\mathscr{C}_{*}\xrightarrow{\phi}J_{*}(2)\forall 1\leq j\leq 6
.
The maps
i_{[2]},\phi_{j}
are finite and
i_{[2]}=[2]\circ\phi_{j}\forall 1\leq j\leq 6
where
[2]:J_{*}(2)\rightarrow J_{*}(2)
is given by
[(L,C,s_{1},\cdots,s_{6})]\mapsto[(L^{\otimes 2}\otimes K_{C}^{\vee},C,s_{1},\cdots,s_{6})]\forall[(L,C,s_{1},\cdots,s_{6})]\in J_{*}(2)
.

We have
\phi_{j*}(A_{*}(\mathscr{C}_{*}))=\mathbb{Q}.[\phi_{j}(\mathscr{C}_{*})]\oplus\mathbb{Q}.e\quad\forall 1\leq j\leq 6
, where
e
is the identity section of the Abelian scheme
J_{*}(2)/M_{*}
since
\phi_{j*}(\mathcal{K})=\phi_{j*}(2\sigma_{j})=2\phi_{j*}(\sigma_{j})=2e\forall j
and
i_{[2]*}(\mathcal{K})=[2]_{*}(e)=e
.

Using
[
9
, Theorem 2.19]
,
A^{1}(J_{*}(2))=\oplus_{-1\leq s\leq 2}A^{1}_{s}(J_{*}(2))
. Let
\phi_{j}(\mathscr{C}_{*})=\Sigma_{-1\leq s\leq 2}a_{j,s}\forall j
. We have

\displaystyle[2]_{*}(\phi_{j}(\mathscr{C}_{*}))=i_{[2]}(\mathscr{C}_{*})\forall j\implies\oplus_{-1\leq s\leq 2}2^{2+s}a_{j,s}=\oplus_{-1\leq s\leq 2}2^{2+s}a_{k,s}\forall 1\leq j,k\leq 6\implies

\displaystyle 2^{2+s}a_{j,s}=2^{2+s}a_{k,s}\forall-1\leq s\leq 2\implies a_{j,s}=a_{k,s}\forall s,j,k\implies[\phi_{j}(\mathscr{C}_{*})]=[\phi_{k}(\mathscr{C}_{*})]\in A^{1}(J_{*}(2))\forall 1\leq j,k\leq 6.

Using the excision sequence on
J_{*}(2)=(J_{*}(2)\setminus Z_{\mathscr{C}}\cup\phi(\mathcal{W}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}\mathscr{C}_{*}))\coprod(Z_{\mathscr{C}}\cup\phi(\mathcal{W}_{*}\times_{M_{*}}\mathscr{C}_{*}))
, we have

\dim A^{i}(J_{*}(2))=0\forall i>2,\dim A^{2}(J_{*}(2))=\dim A^{2}_{0}(J_{*}(2))\leq 1,\dim A^{1}(J_{*}(2))\leq 2.

We show

[\phi_{1}(\mathscr{C}_{*})]\in A^{1}_{0}(J_{*}(2))
. This would further imply

i_{[2]*}(\mathscr{C}_{*})=[2]_{*}([\phi_{1}(\mathscr{C}_{*})])=4[\phi_{1}(\mathscr{C}_{*})].

Consequently, we shall have
\dim A^{1}(J_{*}(2))\leq 1
.

We have that
J_{*}(2)
is a principally polarized Abelian scheme via the
\Theta_{*}
divisor on
J_{*}(1)
.
Hence, the Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}:A_{s}^{p}(J_{*}(2))\simeq A^{2-p+s}_{s}(J_{*}(2))
is defined for all
p,s
, and induces the isomorphisms

(5)

\displaystyle A^{1}_{-1}(J_{*}(2))\simeq A^{0}_{-1}(J_{*}(2))=0,A^{1}_{1}(J_{*}(2))\simeq A^{2}_{1}(J_{*}(2))=0,

(6)

\displaystyle A^{1}_{2}(J_{*}(2))\simeq A^{3}_{2}(J_{*}(2))=0\implies A^{1}(J_{*}(2))=A^{1}_{0}(J_{*}(2))

as claimed and
[Z_{\mathscr{C}}]=[2]_{*}([\phi_{1}(\mathscr{C}_{*})])=4[\phi_{1}(\mathscr{C}_{*})]
.
We have

[\phi_{1}(\mathscr{C}_{*})]=\tau_{2,1}^{*}\Theta_{*}\in A^{1}(J_{*}(2)).

Restricting to a fiber of
J_{*}(1)
over
M_{*}
, shows that
\Theta_{*},\Theta_{*}^{2}\neq 0
in
A^{*}(J_{*}(1))
giving us the claimed form for
A^{*}(J_{*}(d))
for
d\in\mathbb{Z}
.

The claimed form for
A^{*}(J_{2}^{1})
follows from the fact that the divisor
\Theta_{*}\hookrightarrow J_{*}(1)
is the pullback of the universal
\Theta
-divisor. Similarly,

A^{*}(J_{*}(2))=\mathbb{Q}[Z_{\mathscr{C}}]/(Z_{\mathscr{C}}^{3})\implies A^{*}(J_{2}^{2})\simeq\mathbb{Q}[Z]/(Z^{3})

since the cycle
Z_{\mathscr{C}}(=4\Theta_{2})
is the pullback of
Z\in A^{1}(J_{2}^{2})
.
∎

We thus have
A^{*}(J_{2}^{d})
for all values of
d
. These generators and relations are identical to the ones obtained in
[
24
]
.

3.3.
Final Steps

Returning to the original problem, we use that
|2\Theta_{*}|_{\pi}\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(d)
has a finite map to
U(2,2d,2)
.
More conceretely, we have a sequence of maps

|2\Theta_{*}|_{\pi}\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(d)\xrightarrow{q_{\Gamma}}\tilde{U}(2,2d,2)\xrightarrow{q}U(2,2d,2)

where
q
is the map induced by the universality of
U(2,2d,2)
. A straightforward application of the projective bundle formula gives us the following lemma.

Lemma 3.5
.

The product
|2\Theta_{*}|_{\pi}\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(d)
is a projective-bundle over
J_{*}(d)
. Therefore,

A^{*}(|2\Theta_{*}|_{\pi}\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(d))=\mathbb{Q}[A,B]/(A^{4},B^{3})

where
\pi_{\Theta}:|2\Theta_{*}|_{\pi}\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(d)\rightarrow|2\Theta_{*}|_{\pi},\pi_{d}:|2\Theta_{*}|_{\pi}\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(d)\rightarrow J_{*}(d)
are the projection maps and
A:=\pi_{\Theta}^{*}\zeta,B:=\pi_{d}^{*}\Theta_{d},\Theta_{d}=\tau_{d,1}^{*}\Theta_{*}
.

Theorem 3.4
.

Let
\nu\in A^{1}(\tilde{U}(2,2d,2))
be the divisor which pulls back to
\pi_{\Theta}^{*}\zeta
via
q_{\Gamma}^{*}
and
\tilde{Z}_{d}:=det_{d}^{*}\tau_{2d,2}^{*}Z
. We have

A^{*}({U}(2,2d,2))\simeq\mathbb{Q}[\nu,\tilde{Z}_{d}]/(\nu^{4},\tilde{Z}_{d}^{3})

for all
d\in\mathbb{Z}
. Furthermore,
\nu+\dfrac{1}{8}\tilde{Z}_{d}
can be identified with the generalized
\Theta
-divisor if
d
is odd.

Proof.

Applying the projective bundle formula, we use
A^{*}(J_{*}(d))
to determine
A^{*}(U(2,2d,2))
.
We consider the action of
\Gamma
on
|2\Theta_{*}|_{\pi}\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(d)
.

Let
\alpha\in\Gamma
be an arbitrary element, we show that
\alpha.\zeta=\zeta
.

We have
A^{*}(\tilde{U}(2,\mathcal{O},2))=\mathbb{Q}[\zeta]/(\zeta^{4})
and therefore,
\alpha.\zeta=l.\zeta
for some
l\in\mathbb{Q}
. Furthermore

\alpha^{2}=id\implies l^{2}=1\implies l=\pm 1.

It is enough to show that
l\neq-1
. Suppose
l=-1
, then

\alpha.\zeta=-\zeta\implies\alpha.\eta=-\eta

where
\eta
is the restriction of
\zeta
to a fiber over
p=[(C,(p_{i}))]\in M_{*}
. Positivity of
\eta
gives the desired contradiction and we have that
l=1
.
Hence,
\zeta
is invariant under the
\Gamma
-action.

Let
t_{\mathcal{L}}(W)
be the (relative)translation of a divisor
W\rightarrow J_{*}(d)
by
\mathcal{L}\in Pic(\mathscr{C}_{*})
for the abelian scheme
J_{*}(d)\rightarrow M_{*}
. Since

[2]_{*}(\Theta_{d})=[2]_{*}(t_{\mathcal{O}(\sigma_{i}-\sigma_{j})}(\Theta_{d}))\quad\forall 1\leq i,j\leq 6,d\in\mathbb{Z},

invariance of
\Theta_{d}
under the
\Gamma
-action is an immediate consequence of (
6
).

The image of the
\Gamma
-quotient of the pullback of
\Theta_{d}\hookrightarrow J_{*}(d)
to
|2\Theta_{*}|_{\pi}\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(d)
is
\det_{d}^{-1}(\tilde{Z}_{d})\hookrightarrow\tilde{U}(2,2d,2)
as explained in the diagram below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mid 2\Theta_{*}\mid_{\pi}\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(d)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{U}(2,2d,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{*}(d)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{*}(2d).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{\Gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces det_{d}}

That is,

(7)

\pi_{d}^{*}\Theta_{d}=\dfrac{1}{16}q_{\Gamma}^{*}\tilde{Z}_{d}

and
q_{\Gamma}^{*}
is an isomorphism by Lemma
3.5
.
In order to calculate
A^{*}(U(2,2d,2))
, we show that
\nu,\tilde{Z_{d}}
are in the image of
q^{*}
.

We have
\dim A^{1}(U(2,2d,2))=2
by
[
14
, Theorem A.2]
.
Therefore,
q^{*}:A^{1}(U(2,2d,2))\hookrightarrow A^{1}(\tilde{U}(2,2d,2))
is an isomorphism since
q
is finite.

Lastly, we show that, for
d
odd,

q_{\Gamma}^{*}(\nu+\dfrac{1}{8}\tilde{Z}_{d})-q_{\Gamma}^{*}\circ q^{*}(\Theta_{gen})=0.

This is equivalent to showing that

q_{\Gamma}^{*}\circ q^{*}(\Theta_{gen})=\pi_{\Theta}^{*}\zeta+2\pi_{d}^{*}\Theta_{d}.

Applying Theorem
3.2
and Lemma
3.5
,
q_{\Gamma}^{*}\circ q^{*}(\Theta_{gen}),\pi_{\Theta}^{*}\zeta
have identical restrictions over
\tau_{d,0}(e)\hookrightarrow J_{*}(d)
via
\pi_{d}
.
Hence,

q_{\Gamma}^{*}\circ q^{*}(\Theta_{gen})=\pi_{\Theta}^{*}\zeta+a\pi_{d}^{*}\Theta_{d}

for some
a\in\mathbb{Q}
. Restricting to a fiber over
p=[C,(s_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 6}]\in M_{*}
, let
q_{C}:U_{C}(2,\mathcal{O})\times J^{1}(C)\rightarrow U_{C}(2,2)
be the restriction of the quotient map
q_{\Gamma}
over
p
and
h\in A^{1}(U_{C}(2,\mathcal{O}))
be the hyperplane divisor.
We show that

q_{C}^{*}\Theta_{gen,C}=\pi_{U}^{*}h+2\pi_{JC}^{*}\Theta_{C}

where
\pi_{U},\pi_{JC}
are projections onto
U_{C}(2,\mathcal{O}),J^{1}(C)
, respectively, and
\Theta_{gen,C}
is the restriction of
\Theta_{gen}
over
p
. In order to do this, we show that

(8)

\pi_{JC*}(\pi_{U}^{*}h^{3}.q_{C}^{*}\Theta_{gen,C})=2\Theta_{C}\in H^{2}(J^{1}(C),\mathbb{Q})

via the cycle-class map
A^{1}(J^{1}(C))\rightarrow H^{2}(J^{1}(C),\mathbb{Q})
.

Let
p\in C\setminus\{s_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq 6}
be a general point of
C
. Let
[E]\in SU_{C}(2)
be the bundle defined by a non-trivial extension

0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(p-s_{1})\rightarrow E\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(s_{1}-p)\rightarrow 0.

We have
\pi_{U}^{*}h^{3}=[E]\times J^{1}(C)
. The transverse intersection of
[E]\times J^{1}(C),q_{C}^{*}\Theta_{gen,C}
is given by the locus
\{(E,L)|h^{0}(E\otimes L)>0\}_{L\in J^{1}(C)}
. Twisting by
L
, we get

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow L(p-s_{1})\rightarrow E\otimes L\rightarrow L(s_{1}-p)\rightarrow 0.

Therefore,
h^{0}(E\otimes L)>0\iff h^{0}(L(p-s_{1}))>0\text{ or }h^{0}(L(s_{1}-p))>0
.
The cycle
\pi_{JC*}(\pi_{U}^{*}h^{3}.q_{C}^{*}\Theta_{gen,C})
, is hence the union of the loci
\Theta_{C}(p-s_{1}),\Theta_{C}(s_{1}-p)
which are translations of
\Theta_{C}
by the divisors
\mathcal{O}(p-s_{1}),\mathcal{O}(s_{1}-p)
respectively.

The claimed equation (
8
) follows consequently, showing that
a=2
.
∎

We shall provide explicit expressions of these generators in terms of tautological classes in §
5
.
This provides an alternate method to establish
\dim A^{1}(U(2,2d,2))=2
and the fact that
q^{*}
is an isomorphism.

3.4.
The stable locus

We determine
A^{*}(U^{s}(2,2d,2))
using excision and Theorem
3.4
where
U^{s}(2,2d,2)\subset U(2,2d,2)
is the moduli space of stable bundles of rank
2
, and degree
2d
over genus
2
curves.
Let
i^{ss}:U^{sss}(2,2d,2)\hookrightarrow U(2,2d,2)
be the locus of strictly semi-stable bundles in
U(2,2d,2)
. The excision sequence is given by

A^{*-1}(U^{sss}(2,2d,2))\xrightarrow{i_{*}^{ss}}A^{*}(U(2,2d,2))\rightarrow A^{*}(U^{s}(2,2d,2))\rightarrow 0.

We shall show that the image of
i_{*}^{ss}:A^{*-1}(U^{sss}(2,2d,2))\rightarrow A^{*}(U(2,2d,2))
is given by the ideal
(\nu)
which would prove the following theorem.

Theorem 3.5
.

Following the notation in Theorem
3.4
, we have

A^{*}(U^{s}(2,2d,2))\simeq\mathbb{Q}[\tilde{Z}_{d}]/(\tilde{Z}_{d}^{3})

for all
d\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

We use that
q_{\Gamma}^{*},q^{*}
are isomorphisms as established in Theorem
3.4
to get

q_{\Gamma}^{*}\circ q^{*}i_{*}^{ss}(A^{*}({U}^{sss}(2,2d,2)))=i_{J*}^{ss}A^{*}(\tilde{U}^{sss}(2,\mathcal{O},2)\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(d))

where
i_{J}^{ss}
is the inclusion of
\tilde{U}^{sss}(2,\mathcal{O},2)\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(d)\hookrightarrow\tilde{U}(2,\mathcal{O},2)\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(d)
.

The inclusion
i_{J}^{ss}
is the pullback of the inclusion
i_{U}^{ss}:\tilde{U}^{sss}(2,\mathcal{O},2)\hookrightarrow\tilde{U}(2,\mathcal{O},2)
via projection.

Using
[
28
, Theorem 4]
, we know that
(i_{U}^{ss})_{p}
maps onto a quartic surface in
|2\Theta_{\mathscr{C}_{p}}|
over every point
p\in M_{*}
.
Hence,
[\tilde{U}^{ss}(2,\mathcal{O},2)]=4\zeta
in
A^{1}(\tilde{U}(2,\mathcal{O},2))
and
\nu\in Im(i_{*}^{ss})
.

Therefore, it is enough to show that
\pi_{d}^{*}\Theta_{d}^{2}\neq 0
in
A^{*}(\tilde{U}^{s}(2,\mathcal{O},2)\times_{M_{*}}J_{*}(d))
for all
d\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Using the isomorphisms
\{\tau_{d,1}\}_{d\in\mathbb{Z}}
, it is enough to show this inequality just for
d=1
.

We prove by restricting to a fiber over
M_{*}
.

Fixing a
p_{0}=[C,(s_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 6}]\in M_{*}
, it is enough to show that
\pi_{J}^{*}\Theta_{C}^{2}\neq 0
in
A^{2}(U_{C}(2,\mathcal{O})\times J^{1}(C))
which follows from the Künneth formula applied to
H^{4}(U_{C}(2,\mathcal{O})\times J^{1}(C))
via the cycle-class map.
∎

4.
Odd degree

We calculate
A^{*}(U(2,l,2))
for
l
odd in this section.
Suitable twisting by the relative canonical divisor and taking dual if necessary shows that
U(2,l,2)\simeq U(2,3,2)
for all odd integers
l
.
It is therefore enough to calculate
A^{*}(U(2,3,2))
.

4.1.
Bertram’s construction

Let
C
be a curve of genus
2
and
L
be a line bundle on
C
of odd degree. Following
[
5
]
, we use the following notation:

Definition 2
.

Let
U_{C}(2,L)
be the moduli space of stable bundles of rank
2
and determinant
L
, and let
\mathbb{P}_{L}:=\mathbb{P}(Ext^{1}(L,\mathcal{O})^{\vee})
be the projectivized space of extension classes.

We have a rational map
\phi_{L}:\mathbb{P}_{L}\mathbin{\hbox to13.4pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower 1.95277pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{{
{{}
{}{}{}
{}
{}{}{}
\lxSVG@fill}}
}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.98 L 13.01 2.98}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{9.40007pt}{2.15277pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.98 0 C 3.51 0.28 1.11 1.11 -0.55 2.08 L -0.55 -2.08 C 1.11 -1.11 3.51 -0.28 4.98 0}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}U_{C}(2,L)
given by the Poincaré bundle on
C\times\mathbb{P}_{L}
, defined on the subset of extensions
[0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}\rightarrow E\rightarrow L\rightarrow 0]
where
E
is stable.

Definition 3
.

Let
B_{L}:=\{E|h^{0}(C,E)\geq 2\}\hookrightarrow U_{C}(2,L)
be the Brill-Noether locus in
U_{C}(2,L)
of bundles with at least
2
independent sections.
We put
E_{L}:=\Phi_{L}^{-1}(B_{L})
and
Q_{L}
to be the image of
E_{L}
under
\sigma
.

Let
\mathcal{W}_{2,3}^{k}\hookrightarrow U_{C}(2,3)
be the Brill-Noether locus of stable bundles with rank
2
and degree
3
having atleast
k+1
independent sections.
Using
[
20
, Theorem 4.3]
, we have the following:

•

\mathcal{W}_{2,3}^{2}
is empty

•

\mathcal{W}_{2,3}^{1}
is irreducible, smooth and has dimension
3
.

Therefore,
h^{0}(C,E)=2\forall[E]\in B_{L}
.

Theorem 4.1

(
[
5
]
, Theorem 1 and Lemma 4.1)
.

The blowup of
\mathbb{P}_{L}
along
C
, for
deg(L)=3
, is such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{C}(2,L)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{L}}

where the embedding of
C
into
\mathbb{P}_{L}
is given by
C\xrightarrow{|K_{C}+L|}\mathbb{P}_{L}
. We have the following:

(1)

If
x\in C
, then there is a natural isomorphism
\sigma^{-1}(x)\simeq{\mathbb{P}}_{L(-2x)}
, and, when restricted to
\sigma^{-1}(x)
,

\Phi_{L}
coincides with the map

\Phi_{L(-2x)}\otimes\mathcal{O}(x):{\mathbb{P}}_{L(-2x)}\rightarrow U_{C}(2,L)

(2)

The map
\Phi_{L}
resolves the rational map
\phi_{L}
and is defined via a Poincaré bundle on
C\times\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{L}
.

(3)

The exceptional locus of
\Phi_{L}
is
\Phi_{L}^{-1}(B_{L})

and we have

\Phi_{L}^{-1}([E])\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}\quad\forall[E]\in B_{L}.

Lemma 4.1
.

The restriction of
\Phi_{L}
to
E_{L}\cap\sigma^{-1}(C)
maps surjectively onto
B_{L}
.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\delta}\in E_{L}\setminus\sigma^{-1}(C)
be such that
\Phi_{L}(\tilde{\delta})=[E]\in B_{L}
, and let
\delta:=\sigma(\tilde{\delta})
.
We show that
\exists p\in C
and
\gamma\in\sigma^{-1}(p)
such that
\Phi_{L}(\gamma)=[E]
.
By definition,
E
is an extension

0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}\xrightarrow{\theta}E\xrightarrow{\lambda}L\rightarrow 0

such that
h^{0}(C,E)\geq 2
.
Hence,
\exists\alpha\in H^{0}(C,L)\setminus 0
which lifts via
\lambda
. The section
\alpha
of
L
must factor via an inclusion
\mathcal{O}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(p)\xrightarrow{i}L

for some
p\in C
. With slight abuse of notation, we denote the lift by
\alpha
.
Hence, we have the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

We must have
\delta\in ker(H^{1}(C,L^{\vee})\rightarrow H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}))
since
i|_{\mathcal{O}}
lifts to
E
.
But
H^{1}(C,L^{\vee})\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathcal{O})
factors as
H^{1}(C,L^{\vee})\rightarrow H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}(-p))\simeq H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O})
implying a lift
\mathcal{O}(p)\rightarrow E
.

Since
E
is stable, the inclusion
\mathcal{O}(p)\hookrightarrow E
defines a maximal subbundle. Hence,
[E]\in\Phi_{L}(\sigma^{-1}(p))
as claimed.
∎

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
x,y\in\sigma^{-1}(C)
be distinct points such that
\Phi_{L}(x)=\Phi_{L}(y)
. Then
h^{0}(C,L(-p-q))>0
where
p=\sigma(x),q=\sigma(y)
.
Conversely, let
p,q\in C
be distinct points such that
h^{0}(C,L(-p-q))>0
then
\exists!(x,y)\in\sigma^{-1}(p)\times\sigma^{-1}(q)
such that
\Phi_{L}(x)=\Phi_{L}(y)
.

Proof.

For distinct points
x,y\in\sigma^{-1}(C)
such that
\Phi_{L}(x)=\Phi_{L}(y)
, we have the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L(-p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

The composition
s\circ i
is non-zero since
x\neq y
. Therefore,
\mathcal{O}(q)
is a subbundle of
L(-p)
and we have
h^{0}(C,L(-p-q))>0
.

Suppose
p,q\in C
are distinct points such that
h^{0}(C,L(-p-q))>0
. Let
x:=ker(H^{1}(C,L^{\vee}(2p))\rightarrow H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}(p-q)))
where
H^{1}(C,L^{\vee}(2p))\rightarrow H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}(p-q))
is given by twisting
L^{\vee}(2p)
with
L(-p-q)
.
Based on this definition of
x
, we have the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L(-p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

The inclusion
i
lifts to a morphism
\mathcal{O}(q)\rightarrow E
. Using a similar argument as in proof of Lemma
4.1
, we have
[E]\in\Phi_{L}(\sigma^{-1}(q))
.
Let
y\in\sigma^{-1}(q)
be such that
\Phi_{L}(y)=[E]
.
Uniqueness of
x
, and
y
follow from
[
28
, Lemma 3.1]
, and
[
5
, Corollary 4.2]
.
∎

Remark 8
.

Let
p,q,r\in C
be distinct points such that
L\simeq\mathcal{O}(p+q+r)
. The bundles
E_{p,q}
defined using extension classes
x,y
for
p,q,
and
E_{p,r}
defined using extension classes
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}
for
p,r
as in Lemma
4.2
satisfy
E_{p,q}\simeq E_{p,r}
. That is,
x=x^{\prime}
and
\Phi_{L}(x)=\Phi_{L}(y)=\Phi_{L}(y^{\prime})
.

Furthermore,
\Phi_{L}(\tilde{L}_{p,q})=\Phi_{L}(x)
where
\tilde{L}_{p,q}
is the proper transform of the line
L_{p,q}
associated to the blowup
\sigma
.

Lemma 4.3
.

The restriction of
\sigma
to
E_{L}\cap\sigma^{-1}(C)
maps it isomorphically onto
C
if and only if
|L|
is base-point free.
If
L
has a basepoint
p\in C
, then
\sigma^{-1}(p)
maps isomorphically onto
B_{L}
.

Proof.

Let’s assume that
L
is basepoint free. It is enough to show that we have a bijection of points between
E_{L}\cap\sigma^{-1}(C)
and
C
via
\sigma
by Theorem
2.1
.

We show that
\sigma^{-1}(p)\cap E_{L}
consists of a unique point if and only if
p
is not a base-point of
|L|
.
Points in
\sigma^{-1}(p)
are extension classes
0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(p)\rightarrow E\rightarrow L(-p)\rightarrow 0
. For
\Phi_{L}(E)\in B_{L}
, we must have the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L(-p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

The map
i
lifts as shown if and only if
\delta(E)\in ker(H^{1}(C,L^{\vee}(2p))\rightarrow H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}(p)))
where
H^{1}(C,L^{\vee}(2p))\rightarrow H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}(p))
is given by a non-zero section of
L(-p)
.

In the case when
p
is not a base-point of
|L|
,
L(-p)
has a unique nonzero section up to scaling and
dim(ker(H^{1}(C,L^{\vee}(2p))\rightarrow H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}(p))))=1
. This defines the unique extension class
\delta\in\sigma^{-1}(p)
such that
\delta\in E_{L}
.

On the other hand, suppose
|L|
has a base-point
p\in C
. For any
0\neq\tau\in H^{0}(C,L(-p))
with
Z(\tau)=q_{\tau}+\tilde{q}_{\tau}
for some
q_{\tau},\tilde{q}_{\tau}\in C
, we can define the extension class
\delta_{\tau}\in ker(H^{1}(C,L^{\vee}(2p))\rightarrow H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}(p-q_{\tau})))
such that the associated bundle
E_{\tau}
satisfies the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(q_{\tau})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\tau}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{\tau}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L(-p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}

For
\tau,\tau^{\prime}\in H^{0}(C,L(-p))\setminus 0
such that
\tau\not\in<\tau^{\prime}>
, the defined extension classes
\delta_{\tau},\delta_{\tau^{\prime}}\in H^{1}(C,L^{\vee}(2p))
are distinct.
Thus, for every non-zero section
\tau
of
L(-p)
, we have a unique extension class
\delta_{\tau}:=ker(H^{1}(C,L^{\vee}(2p))\rightarrow H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}(p)))
defining a bundle
E_{\tau}\in B_{L}
via
\Phi_{L}
.

Hence, we have
\sigma^{-1}(p)\hookrightarrow E_{L}
if
p
is a basepoint of
L
.
On the other hand, let
E\in B_{L}
. By Lemma
4.1
,
q\in\sigma(\Phi_{L}^{-1}([E]))
for some
q\in C
.
If
q\neq p
, then
h^{0}(C,L(-p-q))>0
and since
q
is not a basepoint of
L
,
\Phi_{L}(E_{L}\cap\sigma^{-1}(q))=[E]
. By Lemma
4.2
, we have that
[E]\in\Phi_{L}(\sigma^{-1}(p))
. Therefore,
\Phi_{L}
induces a bijection between
\sigma^{-1}(p)
and
B_{L}
. Using Theorem
2.1
, we have the claimed isomorphism.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

The Brill-Noether locus,
B_{L}
, is a smooth rational curve.

Proof.

If
L
has a basepoint
p\in C
, then
B_{L}\simeq\sigma^{-1}(p)\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}
by Lemma
4.3
.

If
L
is basepoint free, we have
C\simeq E_{L}\cap\sigma^{-1}(C)
by Lemma
4.3
.
Thus,
C\simeq E_{L}\cap\sigma^{-1}(C)\xrightarrow{\Phi_{L}}B_{L}
is surjective and non-constant by Lemma
4.1
, and Lemma
4.2
.
Hence,
B_{L}
is irreducible and has dimension
1
. We identify the restriction of
\Phi_{L}
to
E_{L}\cap\sigma^{-1}(C)
with the induced map
C\rightarrow B_{L}
.

Let
\tilde{B}_{L}
be the normalization of
B_{L}
. The restricted morphism,
\Phi_{L}|_{C}
, then factors via
C\rightarrow\tilde{B}_{L}\rightarrow B_{L}
.
For
p,q,r\in C
distinct such that
L\sim\mathcal{O}(p+q+r)
, we have
\Phi_{L}(p)=\Phi_{L}(q)=\Phi_{L}(r)
by Remark
8
.

Since
\tilde{B}_{L}\rightarrow B_{L}
is birational,
C\rightarrow\tilde{B}_{L}
is a degree
3
map between smooth curves and
\tilde{B}_{L}
has genus at most
1
by the Riemann-Hurwitz Theorem.

The map
\Phi_{L}|_{C}
has identical fibers to
C\xrightarrow{|L|}\mathbb{P}^{1}
given by the linear system of
L
by Lemma
4.2
, which has at least
4
branch points. That is, there are at least
4
points in
B_{L}
whose fibers have less than
3
points.
Therefore,
g(\tilde{B}_{L})=0
and
\tilde{B}_{L}\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
A general fiber of
\tilde{\Phi}_{L}
coincides with a general fiber of
\Phi_{L}|_{C}
by Lemma
4.2
.
Thus,
\tilde{\Phi}_{L}
is given by the linear system
|L|
.

This shows that distinct points
a,b\in\tilde{B}_{L}
must map to distinct points in
B_{L}
by Lemma
4.2
. Therefore,
\tilde{B}_{L}\simeq B_{L}
and
B_{L}
is a smooth rational curve.
∎

Remark 9
.

If
L
has a basepoint
p
then
Q_{L}\setminus p\simeq E_{L}\setminus\sigma^{-1}(p)
via
\sigma
by Lemma
4.3
.
For the induced morphism
\phi_{L}:C\setminus p\hookrightarrow Q_{L}\setminus p\xrightarrow{\Phi_{L}}B_{L}
, we have
\phi_{L}(q)=\phi_{L}(r)
if and only if
K_{C}\sim q+r
by Lemma
4.2
. Hence,
\phi_{L}=\alpha|_{C\setminus p}
and the extension of
\phi_{L}
to
C
is therefore given by
\alpha
where
\alpha
is the
g^{1}_{2}
-morphism of
C
.

Theorem 4.2
.

If
L
has a basepoint
p\in C
, then
Q_{L}
is a quadric cone in
\mathbb{P}_{L}
with vertex at
p
. On the other hand if
L
is basepoint free,
E_{L}
maps isomorphically onto
Q_{L}
via
\sigma
and
Q_{L}
is a smooth quadric surface in
\mathbb{P}_{L}
.

Proof.

By
[
5
, Definition-Claim 4.6]
,
Q_{L}
is a quadric hypersurface in
\mathbb{P}_{L}
.
In the case when
L
is basepoint free,
E_{L}
maps isomorphically onto
Q_{L}
via
\sigma
by Lemma
4.3
.
Since
E_{L}\rightarrow B_{L}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration by Theorem
4.1
,
E_{L}
is a Hirzebruch surface by Lemma
4.4
.
Thus, it must be a smooth quadric surface.

On the other hand, let
p\in C
be a basepoint of
L
and
q\in C\setminus p
.
We have
p,q,\overline{q}
are collinear in
\mathbb{P}_{L}
for any
q\in C
where
\overline{q}
is the hyperelliptic conjugate of
q
by Lemma
4.2
,.
Therefore,
Q_{L}
consists of lines joining triples of points
\{(p,q,\overline{q})|q\in C\}
. Consequently,
Q_{L}=C_{p}(C)
where
C_{p}(C)
is the cone obtained by lines joining points in
C
with
p
.
∎

Remark 10
.

For any divisor
D
on
C
, put
\mathbb{P}^{D}:=\mathbb{P}H^{0}(C,D)^{\vee}
. For
L
basepoint free, the Segre embedding
s_{L}:\mathbb{P}^{K}\times\mathbb{P}^{L}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}_{L}
is given by the isomorphism
H^{0}(C,K_{C})\otimes H^{0}(C,L)\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{0}(C,K_{C}\otimes L)
.
The maps
C\xrightarrow{|K_{C}|}\mathbb{P}^{K},C\xrightarrow{|L|}\mathbb{P}^{L}
induce the morphism
C\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{K}\times\mathbb{P}^{L}\xrightarrow{s}\mathbb{P}_{L}
which coincides with the embedding of
C
into
\mathbb{P}_{L}
. Hence,
Q_{L}=Im(s)
is the unique quadric containing
C
in
\mathbb{P}_{L}
.
Furthermore, the induced map
C\rightarrow B_{L}
is given by the linear system
|L|
and therefore,
\Phi_{L}|_{E_{L}}:E_{L}\simeq Q_{L}\rightarrow B_{L}
can be identified with the projection onto
\mathbb{P}^{L}
.

If
L
has a basepoint
p
, the map
E_{L}\rightarrow B_{L}
is still a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration over a smooth rational curve. Therefore,
E_{L}
is a Hirzebruch surface.
The map
E_{L}\rightarrow Q_{L}
is a birational map which contracts
\sigma^{-1}(p)\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Hence,
E_{L}=bl_{C}Q_{L}\hookrightarrow\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{L}
is the strict transform of
Q_{L}
. We further have the morphism
E_{L}\rightarrow bl_{p}Q_{L}
by the universal property of blowups, which is a bijection on closed points.
Therefore,
E_{L}\simeq bl_{p}Q_{L}

Remark 11
.

Irrespective of the basepoint freeness of
L
, we have the induced morphism
bl_{C}(\mathbb{P}_{L})\rightarrow bl_{B_{L}}(U_{C}(2,L))
which induces a bijection on closed points. Therefore, we have

bl_{C}(\mathbb{P}_{L})\simeq bl_{B_{L}}(U_{C}(2,L)).

4.2.
Generalizing to
U(2,3,2)

With slight abuse of notation for this subsection, let
\mathcal{L}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}\xrightarrow{\pi}S
be a family of smooth genus
2
curves with a line bundle
\mathcal{L}
of relative degree
3
.

The analogue of the extension space is
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{L}}:=\underline{Proj}_{S}\operatorname{Sym}^{\bullet}(R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathcal{L}^{\vee})^{\vee}
with
\pi_{\mathcal{L}}:\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{L}}\rightarrow S
the induced morphism.

By Serre duality for the family
\pi
, we have
(R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathcal{L}^{\vee})^{\vee}\simeq\pi_{*}(K_{\pi}\otimes\mathcal{L})
.

The surjection
\pi^{*}\pi_{*}(K_{\pi}\otimes\mathcal{L})\twoheadrightarrow(K_{\pi}\otimes\mathcal{L})
defines an embedding
\mathscr{C}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{L}}
. We shall denote the image of this embedding by
B^{0}(\mathcal{L})
.

Let
\sigma_{\pi}:\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{L}}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{L}}
be the blowup of
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{L}}
along
B^{0}(\mathcal{L})
and
\beta:\mathscr{C}\times_{S}B^{0}(\mathcal{L})\rightarrow S
be the composed morphism. We define a Poincaré bundle on
\mathscr{C}\times_{S}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{L}}
that restricts to the Poincaré bundle used in Theorem
4.1
over each fiber of
\beta
.

Let
\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{L}}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}\times_{S}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{L}}
be the Poincaré bundle given by
\mathcal{L},\pi
using
[
34
, Remark 1]
.

Lemma 4.5
.

We have the following unique lift
f_{\mathcal{L}}
over
\mathscr{C}\times_{S}B^{0}(\mathcal{L})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{B^{0}(\mathcal{L})}^{*}(\mathcal{O}(1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{L}}|_{\mathscr{C}\times_{S}B^{0}(\mathcal{L})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathscr{C}}^{*}\mathcal{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{L}}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{L}}}

where we identify
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{L}}}(1)
with its restriction to
B^{0}(\mathcal{L})
,
\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{L}}^{0}:=\pi_{B^{0}(\mathcal{L})}^{*}(\mathcal{O}(1))\otimes\mathcal{O}(\Delta)
and
\Delta\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}\times_{S}B^{0}(\mathcal{L})
is the diagonal.

Proof.

We show the existence of this lift
f_{\mathcal{L}}
and then prove uniqueness. Let

\mathcal{F}:=\pi_{\mathscr{C}}^{*}\mathcal{L}^{\vee}\otimes\pi_{B^{0}(\mathcal{L})}^{*}(\mathcal{O}(1)),\mathcal{G}:=\pi_{\mathscr{C}}^{*}\mathcal{L}^{\vee}\otimes\pi_{B^{0}(\mathcal{L})}^{*}(\mathcal{O}(1))\otimes\mathcal{O}(\Delta)

and let
\partial\in H^{1}(\mathscr{C}\times_{S}B^{0}(\mathcal{L}),\mathcal{F})
be the extension class of
\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{L}}|_{\mathscr{C}\times_{S}B^{0}(\mathcal{L})}
.

Let
U
be an arbitrary affine open in
S
, and let
CB_{U}:=\beta^{-1}(U)
. We show that
\partial|_{CB_{U}}\in ker(H^{1}(CB_{U},\mathcal{F}|_{CB_{U}})\rightarrow H^{1}(CB_{U},\mathcal{G}|_{CB_{U}}))
.

Since
U
is affine,
H^{1}(CB_{U},\mathcal{F}|_{CB_{U}})\simeq\Gamma(U,R^{1}\beta_{U*}(\mathcal{F}|_{CB_{U}})),H^{1}(CB_{U},\mathcal{G}|_{CB_{U}})\simeq\Gamma(U,R^{1}\beta_{U*}(\mathcal{G}|_{CB_{U}}))
. By Cohomology and Base Change, we have

H^{1}(CB_{U},\mathcal{F}|_{CB_{U}})\otimes k(s)\simeq H^{1}(C_{s}\times B^{0}(L_{s}),\mathcal{F}_{s}),H^{1}(CB_{U},\mathcal{G}|_{CB_{U}})\otimes k(s)\simeq H^{1}(C_{s}\times B^{0}(L_{s}),\mathcal{G}_{s})\forall s\in S

As explained in
[
5
, Definition-Claim 3.4]
,

\displaystyle\partial_{s}\in ker(H^{1}(C_{s}\times B^{0}(L_{s}),\mathcal{F}_{s})\rightarrow H^{1}(C_{s}\times B^{0}(L_{s}),\mathcal{G}_{s}))\forall s\in U\implies

\displaystyle\partial\otimes k(s)\in ker(H^{1}(CB_{U},\mathcal{F}|_{CB_{U}})\otimes k(s)\rightarrow H^{1}(CB_{U},\mathcal{G}|_{CB_{U}})\otimes k(s))\forall s\in U\implies

\displaystyle\partial\in ker(H^{1}(CB_{U},\mathcal{F}|_{CB_{U}})\rightarrow H^{1}(CB_{U},\mathcal{G}|_{CB_{U}})).

Therefore, we have local lifts
f_{\mathcal{L},U}:\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{L}}|_{CB_{U}}\rightarrow\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{L}}^{0}|_{CB_{U}}
for the morphism
\pi_{B^{0}(\mathcal{L}|_{\mathscr{C}_{U}})}^{*}(\mathcal{O}(1))\xrightarrow{\otimes\Delta_{U}}\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{L}}^{0}|_{CB_{U}}
over every open affine
U\subset S
. Next we show that these lifts
f_{\mathcal{L},U}
are unique.

Suppose there are lifts
f_{\mathcal{L},U}^{1},f_{\mathcal{L},U}^{2}:\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{L}}|_{CB_{U}}\rightarrow\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{L}}^{0}|_{CB_{U}}
. Then
f_{\mathcal{L},U}^{1}-f_{\mathcal{L},U}^{2}
lifts to a morphism
\pi_{\mathscr{C}_{U}}^{*}\mathcal{L}|_{\mathscr{C}_{U}}\rightarrow\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{L}}^{0}|_{CB_{U}}
.

We show that
\operatorname{Hom}(\pi_{\mathscr{C}_{U}}^{*}\mathcal{L}|_{\mathscr{C}_{U}},\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{L}}^{0}|_{CB_{U}})=0
to conclude that these local lifts
f_{\mathcal{L},U}
are unique over every open affine
U
. It is enough to show that
H^{0}(CB_{U},\pi_{\mathscr{C}_{U}}^{*}\mathcal{L}^{\vee}|_{\mathscr{C}_{U}}\otimes\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{L}}^{0}|_{CB_{U}})=0
.

Let
t\in H^{0}(CB_{U},\pi_{\mathscr{C}_{U}}^{*}\mathcal{L}^{\vee}|_{\mathscr{C}_{U}}\otimes\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{L}}^{0}|_{CB_{U}})
be a section. For a point
b\in B^{0}(\mathcal{L}|_{\mathscr{C}_{U}})
with
\pi_{\mathcal{L}}(b)=s\in U
,
t|_{\mathscr{C}_{s}\times b}\in H^{0}(\mathscr{C}_{s},\mathcal{L}|_{\mathscr{C}_{s}}^{\vee})=0
. Therefore,

t|_{(CB_{U})_{b}}=0\forall b\in B^{0}(\mathcal{L}|_{\mathscr{C}_{U}})
. In other words,
t=0
and
consequently, we have
H^{0}(CB_{U},\pi_{\mathscr{C}_{U}}^{*}\mathcal{L}^{\vee}|_{\mathscr{C}_{U}}\otimes\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{L}}^{0}|_{CB_{U}})=0
proving the uniqueness of the lift
f_{\mathcal{L},U}
for all open affines
U\subset S
.

By the uniqueness of the lifts,
f_{\mathcal{L},U}|_{CB_{V}}=f_{\mathcal{L},V}
for arbitrary affine opens
V\subset U\subset S
. Hence, the lifts
\{f_{\mathcal{L},U}\}_{U}
patch together to define a lift
\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{L}}|_{\mathscr{C}\times_{S}B^{0}(\mathcal{L})}\xrightarrow{f_{\mathcal{L}}}\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{L}}^{0}
as claimed.
The uniqueness of
f_{\mathcal{L}}
follows from its construction.
∎

Theorem 4.3
.

There is a bundle
\mathscr{E}^{1}
on
\mathscr{C}\times_{S}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{L}}
which restricts to the Poincaré bundle on
\mathscr{C}_{s}\times\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{L}_{s}}
, as defined in Theorem
4.1
, for all
s\in S
. That is, the bundle
\mathscr{E}^{1}
defines a map
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{L}}\xrightarrow{\Phi_{\mathcal{L}}}{U}(2,3,2)
.

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{E}^{0}:=(1,\sigma_{\pi})^{*}\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{L}}
be the pullback of
\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{L}}
over
